Hawkes autoregressive processes: a new model for multiscale and heterogeneous processes
T.Théo Leblanc

Abstract
Both Hawkes processes and autoregressive processes depend on linear functionals of their past while modelling different types of data. As different datasets obtained through the recording of the same phenomena may be heterogeneous and occur at different timescales, it is important to study multiscale and heterogenous processes, such as those obtained by combining Hawkes and autoregressive processes. In this paper, we present probabilistic results for this new Hawkes autoregressive (HAR) model, including the existence of a stationary version, a cluster representation, exponential moments and asymptotic behaviour. We also derive statistical results for estimating interactions, extending the well-known LASSO estimation method to Hawkes Autoregressive (HAR) processes.

, 62M10
; secondary 60G10
, 62H12

keywords

Hawkes processes
, Autoregressive processes
, Cluster representation

1
Introduction

1.1
Motivations

Hawkes processes, introduced by Hawkes in 1971 in
[
30
]
, are self-interacting point processes modelling the continuous arrival of points over time. In the multivariate case,
points
are emitted by several
nodes
. The occurrence of each node may alter the future probability of a point being emitted by any node. Hawkes processes can be self-exciting, self-inhibiting, or both simultaneously. This flexibility enables Hawkes processes to model a wide range of phenomena, such as earthquake occurrences
[
46
]
, financial markets
[
24
]
, epidemiology
[
43
]
and the activity of the neural network of the brain
[
38
,
9
,
26
,
49
]
, to cite but a few. Concerning applications in neuroscience, Hawkes processes model a network of neurons where each node is a neuron and the points of a given node correspond to the spiking times of the associated neuron. With this modelling point of view, the dependency of the process on the past occurrences models, in particular, synaptic integration. Hawkes processes have been the focus of very active research in the past decades, see for example
[
11
,
2
,
19
]
.

Autoregressive (AR) processes, as opposed to Hawkes processes, are discrete time processes with continuous values. However, Autoregressive processes depend on their past as Hawkes processes, and this is why Hawkes processes are often referred to as autoregressive point processes. Indeed, the value at time
t\in\mathds{Z}
of an AR process depends on a function of the previous values at time
s<t
and on an additive noise term,
[
10
,
53
]
. Depending on the function that codes the past dependency, AR processes may be self-exciting, self-inhibiting, or both simultaneously, as for Hawkes processes
[
34
]
. Moreover, as for Hawkes processes, AR processes have a multivariate extension, often denoted VAR or MVAR, for (multivariate) vector autoregressive processes, where several time series depend on each other and on their past
[
42
]
. We call these discrete random sequences
chains
in the present article. The simplicity and versatility of AR processes make them popular and widely used in many fields, such as economics and finance, see
[
25
,
7
]
. More recently, AR processes have been used in many other fields. One such field is neuroscience, where EEG (electroencephalogram) data and brain rhythms can be modelled using AR processes
[
39
,
59
]
.

Hawkes processes model continuous arrival times of events whereas AutoRegressive processes are able to model discrete time series of real values, which makes Hawkes processes and AR processes highly complementary since they have similar past dependency while being of different nature. Since experiments may produce simultaneously different type of data it is natural to seek for heterogenous models, i.e. combining processes of different nature such as a continuous time process and a discrete time process. Another important observation is that data may exist on different timescales, for example if two simultaneous phenomena are recorded with different frequencies of measurements. Thus, it is natural to look for a model also able to handle multiscale data. The main goal of the present article is to introduce and study a new model combining a Hawkes process and an autoregressive process with a multiscale architecture.

Hawkes processes have already been combined with other processes. Indeed, in
[
21
]
and
[
1
]
, the authors combine a diffusion and a Hawkes process to create an interesting heterogeneous model where points of the Hawkes process are introduced in a stochastic diffusion. However, the diffusion does not interact with the Hawkes process; therefore, only one cross interaction is taken into account. Hawkes diffusion models have many applications in finance
[
31
]
.

Multiscale processes naturally arise in the context of wavelets and lead to AR processes defined on trees
[
5
,
18
]
or autoregressive modelling for each component of the wavelet transform
[
37
]
. However, AR processes on trees are often non causal, as they introduce interactions between ascending or descending scales regardless of causality
[
5
,
18
]
. It is also possible to introduce a multiscale architecture into Hawkes processes or in AR processes by parameterising interactions with varying memory parameters, see
[
3
]
for Hawkes processes and
[
4
]
for AR processes. These models are, however, homogeneous since they do not combine Hawkes and AR processes. In the present article, we instead adopt the approach used in
[
54
]
: to introduce a multiscale nature to AR processes we consider a multivariate AR process
\bm{X}=(X^{1},\cdots,X^{P})
where each time series
X^{k}=(X^{k}_{t})_{t\in\tau_{k}\mathds{Z}}
has its own temporal embedding
\tau_{k}\mathds{Z}
where
\tau_{k}
is the period.

A multiscale and heterogeneous model was introduced in
[
54
]
to describe the coupled interactions between the activity of individual neurons and brain rhythms, leading to a complete description of brain activity and functional connectivity. The model mixes discrete time Hawkes processes and multiscale AR processes. This new model is interesting in itself as well as for the perspectives it brings to neuroscience. In the present article, we aim to build upon this by relaxing the discrete nature of the Hawkes process. To this end, we introduce a model consisting of a continuous time Hawkes process and a multiscale autoregressive process in reciprocal interactions, named HAR process for
Hawkes Autoregressive process
. In
[
54
]
the authors mostly develop the modelling side, the discrete time nature making the mathematical work quite straightforward. Having a continuous time Hawkes process instead of a discrete one reveals all the theoretical difficulties which were not addressed in
[
54
]
such as points accumulations in finite time. More importantly we obtain sharper conditions for the existence of the process in continuous time. The next section explains how to naturally combine a Hawkes process and an autoregressive process to obtain a Hawkes Autoregressive (HAR) process.

1.2
Heuristic for HAR processes

We provide an heuristic to combine Hawkes and Autoregressive processes in order to obtain HAR processes. For this heuristic we only focus on the one dimensional case.

Hawkes processes:
A Hawkes process
S
is defined through its stochastic predictable intensity. The intensity at time
t
can be viewed as the infinitesimal probability of having a point between
t
and
t+dt
while knowing the past before
t
,

\lambda_{t}dt=\mathds{E}\left[dS_{t}\ \middle|\ \mathcal{F}_{t-}\right].

For Hawkes processes, the intensity depends on the past as follows:

\lambda_{t}=\phi\left(\displaystyle\int^{t-}_{-\infty}h(t-s)dS_{s}\right),

where
\phi
and
h
are two functions. Unlike
h
, we assume
\phi
to be non negative and non decreasing. The function
h
models the self interaction of the point process
S
on itself. The function
\phi
allows for the introduction of nonlinear dependency,
[
11
,
30
]
. Thus one can write

\mathds{E}\left[dS_{t}\ \middle|\ \mathcal{F}_{t-}\right]=\phi\left(\displaystyle\int^{t-}_{-\infty}h(t-s)dS_{s}\right)dt.

(1.1)

Autoregressive processes:
For AR processes, the current value of the observation can be calculated through the past ones, up to some noise. AR processes can be defined by the following equation

W_{k}=\sum_{r\geq 1}a_{r}W_{k-r}+\varepsilon_{k},

(1.2)

where
a_{r}
are fixed coefficients for positive integers
r
and
\varepsilon_{k}
is a random noise independent of the
W_{k^{\prime}}
with
k^{\prime}<k

[
53
,
10
]
. The function
a:r\in\mathds{N}^{*}\mapsto a_{r}=:a(r)
, where
\mathds{N}^{*}
stands for the positive integers, models the past dependency. One can generalise AR processes by allowing a more complex past dependency leading to

W_{k}=\Psi(W_{k-1},W_{k-2},\cdots)+\varepsilon_{k},

with
\Psi
a function. In order to obtain the analogue of (
1.1
) we stick to the following level of generality,

W_{k}=\psi\left(\sum_{k^{\prime}<k}a(k-k^{\prime})\kappa(W_{k^{\prime}})\right)+\varepsilon_{k},

(1.3)

where
\psi,\ a
and
\kappa
are given functions. Taking
\psi=\kappa=\id
leads to the classical AR model described in (
1.2
). The function
\psi
allows the introduction of some nonlinearity and is the analogue of the function
\phi
in (
1.1
). The function
\kappa
allows for more flexibility and even more nonlinearity, for example, one can use
\kappa(x)=|x|
or
\kappa(x)=x_{+}
.

By denoting
{D}
the counting measure on the set of the integers, (
1.3
) becomes

W_{k}=\psi\left(\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{k-}a(k-k^{\prime})\kappa(W_{k^{\prime}})d{D}_{k^{\prime}}\right)+\varepsilon_{k}.

(1.4)

In particular, we obtain from (
1.4
) the following equality:

\mathds{E}\left[W_{k}\ \middle|\ \mathcal{F}_{k-1}\right]=\psi\left(\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{k-}a(k-k^{\prime})\kappa(W_{k^{\prime}})d{D}_{k^{\prime}}\right).

(1.5)

General Hawkes and AR processes have very similar structures, see (
1.1
) and (
1.5
). So it is easy to combine them. For this purpose we add cross interaction terms in the intensity equation and in the AR equation, leading to the following system of equations.

\left\{\begin{aligned} \lambda_{t}&=\phi\left(\displaystyle\int^{t-}_{-\infty}h(t-s)dS_{s}+\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t-}h_{W\to S}(t-k)\kappa_{S}(W_{k})d{D}_{k}\right)\\
W_{k}&=\psi\left(\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{k-}a(k-k^{\prime})\kappa(W_{k^{\prime}})d{D}_{k^{\prime}}+\displaystyle\int^{k-}_{-\infty}h_{S\to W}(k-s)dS_{s}\right)+\varepsilon_{k},\end{aligned}\right.

(1.6)

where
h_{W\to S},\ \kappa_{S},\ h_{S\to W}
are some functions. Equations (
1.6
) provides the heuristic definition of HAR processes.

1.3
Probabilistic contributions and related works

In the present article, we focus on deriving the following probabilistic results on HAR processes: existence of stationary HAR processes; cluster representation; exponential moments; and, finally, rate of convergence of HAR processes to the stationary distribution, leading to coupling
à la Berbee
and concentration inequalities. These probabilistic results are crucial, in particular, to prove theoretical guaranties for estimation procedures.

Stationary HAR processes.
Brémaud and Massoulié
[
11
]
have shown existence and uniqueness of stationary Hawkes processes if the spectral radius of the matrix gathering the
L^{1}
norms of the interaction functions is smaller than
1
. An AR
(\infty)
process with autoregressive coefficients
a_{k}
for
k\geq 1
is stationary and causal if for example
|a_{1}|+|a_{2}|+\cdots<1
, condition, which also extends to a spectral radius condition in the multivariate case, see
[
42
]
. We extend both these results by stating existence and uniqueness of stationary HAR processes under the condition that the matrix gathering the
L^{1}
norms of the interaction functions (including both homogeneous and cross interactions) has a spectral radius smaller than
1
, see Theorem
3.9
. To prove this result, we use a Picard iteration, which is a classical method in the Hawkes framework
[
11
]
.

Cluster representation.
The cluster representation for linear Hawkes processes was introduced by Hawkes and Oakes in
[
29
]
. This representation is a powerful tool for performing in-depth calculations, leading to results such as mixing properties
[
8
]
and exponential moments
[
40
]
. In Theorem
4.5
we derive a cluster representation for
linear
HAR. This extends the cluster representation of linear Hawkes processes. We obtain this cluster representation by solving the autoregressive equation to decouple the equations. HAR clusters are generated by bivariate functions
h(s,t)
, depending on both the past occurrence time,
s
, and the present time,
t
, whereas for classical Hawkes processes we only consider
h(s,t)=h(t-s)
, see (
1.1
). Let us mention that in
[
52
]
, the authors studied
locally-stationary Hawkes processes
: a collection of point processes indexed by
T>0
with cluster functions given by
h(s,t/T)
and base rates depending on
t/T
, with a focus on the dependence on T of the moments and Laplace transform. Limit theorems have been proved for similar processes where
h(s,t/T)=g(t/T)\phi(t-s)
in
[
20
]
. For HAR processes, we are far from the framework of
[
52
,
20
]
since cluster representation for HAR processes leads to
1
-periodic bivariate functions satisfying
h(s,t)=h(s+1,t+1)
. The clusters we obtain, are a particular case of clusters, for which sharp bounds on their exponential moments are derived in
[
41
]
.

Exponential moments.
In
[
40
]
, exponential moments for Hawkes processes were proved using a cluster representation under minimal assumptions, namely sub-critical clusters, which is equivalent to requiring the spectral radius of the offspring matrix to be smaller than
1
. Previously, exponential moments for Hawkes processes were derived with strong assumptions on the decay of the interaction functions, see
[
50
,
28
]
, or with strong conditions on the offspring matrix such as requiring its spectral norm to be smaller than
1
. In Theorem
5.3
, in order to prove exponential moments for HAR processes under the condition of sub-critical clusters, we borrow and adapt the ideas from the proof in
[
40
]
to our case involving the autoregressive part. Since the clusters are generated by bivariate functions depending on both
s
and
t
, we also use the results recently developed in
[
41
]
about these type of clusters.

Rate of convergence and stability.
Given a dynamic, for an initial condition
C_{0}
and
N^{C_{0}}
the process following the dynamic with initial condition
C_{0}
, the dynamic is said to be stable at
C_{0}
if the process
N^{C_{0}}
shifted by
t
units of time converges in distribution as
t\to\infty
to the stationary process
N
following the dynamic, see
[
11
]
. Brémaud and Massoulié
[
11
]
proved stability for multivariate nonlinear Hawkes processes under spectral radius condition for existence of the stationary process. This result is only a convergence in distribution and more precise statements arise from the study of renewal times and regenerative properties for Hawkes processes, see
[
17
]
and
[
27
]
, allowing to decompose Hawkes processes in independent random cycles and revealing a recurrent Markov process. By using a convolution equation, Clinet and Yoshida
[
16
]
even proved convergence rates; in the case of exponential Hawkes processes, the
L^{1}
convergence rate is also exponential. In Theorem
6.3
, we prove similar results for HAR processes. More precisely, for exponentially decaying interaction functions, we prove that two HAR processes, constructed on the same randomness with two different initial conditions, converge almost surely toward each others as time goes to infinity. We also prove exponential convergence rates in
L^{p}
.

Coupling and concentration possibilities.
Our last probabilistic contribution is about a coupling and applications to concentration for HAR processes. Concentration results for Hawkes processes can be achieved using various methods, such as renewal times
[
17
]
, mixing properties
[
8
]
or Kalikow decomposition
[
48
,
47
]
. For HAR processes, however, we turn to a coupling method,
à la Berbee
, inspired by
[
6
]
. This idea has been widely used to obtain concentration results for stationary time series see for example
[
58
,
23
,
51
]
. For Hawkes processes, the idea is to introduce a coupling consisting of independent pieces, and such that the coupling is sufficiently close to the initial process, see
[
50
]
. To construct the coupling we use non stationary HAR processes started at different times. For example, a HAR process
X^{t_{0}}
started at
t_{0}
is independent of the past before
t_{0}
and, due to the asymptotic limit discussed above, is close to the stationary process if it has evolved for a sufficiently long time. Thus the pieces constituting the process look like
(X^{t_{0}})\big|_{[t_{0}+\ell,t_{0}+2\ell)}
for
\ell
large enough, see Definition
6.5
. This coupling is used, in particular, in the statistical part, Section
7
, to prove concentration of the Gram matrix.

1.4
Statistical contributions and related works

In the present article, in addition to the probabilistic study of HAR processes, we propose a statistical method based on LASSO to estimate the interactions of HAR processes. LASSO was introduced by
[
56
]
and consists in minimizing a penalized contrast by an
L^{1}
penalty. We refer to
[
13
]
for general review.

Related works about estimation for Hawkes processes.
Numerous techniques have been developed to estimate the interaction functions of Hawkes processes. One example is Bayesian inference, where concentration rates are derived for the posterior distribution leading to non-parametric estimation of the parameters and consistency in the graph adjacency of the interactions, see
[
22
]
in the linear case and in nonlinear case,
[
55
]
. Another possibility is to use the maximum likelihood (MLE), as in
[
9
]
, where the authors first estimate the adjacency graph of a nonlinear (ReLu link function) Hawkes process with MLE and thresholding, and then re-estimate non-zero interaction functions with MLE. About the adjacency graph of interactions of a linear Hawkes process, in
[
15
]
the authors recover a superset of it, by estimating the covariances. Estimation based on least squares and the LASSO has been introduced for a large class of point processes including Hawkes processes in
[
28
]
. The authors restrict the parameter space to linear combinations of a fixed dictionary of functions with bounded support and derive an Oracle inequality for the LASSO estimator of the interaction functions of a linear Hawkes process. Further work has been carried out in the linear case such as
[
3
]
where the dictionary has a tensor structure and in
[
36
]
where the base rates depend on covariates and global parameters. In
[
36
]
the authors also provide a debiasing method. In the nonlinear case, especially for Hawkes processes with ReLu link functions, LASSO estimator have been the subject of
[
14
]
with strong assumptions on the intensity and
[
38
]
with applications in the recovery of functional connectivity in neural networks in the brain.

Related works about estimation for Autoregressive processes.
Estimation of parameters for autoregressive processes (AR) and more generally MA, ARMA and ARIMA processes is discussed in the review
[
35
]
. Various techniques exist, such as MLE, as discussed in, for example,
[
39
]
, with applications to EEG data, or ordinary least squares (OLS). When the OLS is penalised with an
L^{1}
penalty, a LASSO estimator is obtained. In
[
45
]
, the authors provide convergence rates for the LASSO estimator of the coefficients of an AR
(p)
process with
p=O(\log(n))
, where
n
is the number of observations. The rates depend on the penalty weights, but are approximately in
\sqrt{p/n}
. In the multivariate case, for vector autoregressive processes, the authors of
[
32
]
use the LASSO to select and estimate non-zero coefficients in a faster way than with other model selection procedures. They also prove a convergence rate of order
1/\sqrt{n}
for the LASSO estimator.

LASSO estimation for HAR processes.
To estimate the interaction functions of linear HAR processes we use the LASSO procedure to select non-zero parameters and achieve sparse estimation, even when the dimension is large. This approach was also adopted in
[
54
]
for discrete time HAR processes. We combine both classical least squares contrast for linear Hawkes processes and for autoregressive processes and an
L^{1}
penalty with adaptative weights. Adaptative weights, see
[
60
,
33
]
, take into account the heterogeneity of the coefficients, thereby improving the performance of the LASSO. Here, as in
[
28
,
36
,
54
]
we allow the weights of the
L^{1}
penalty to depend on the data in order to estimate the variability of each coordinate of the estimator. Following the steps of
[
28
,
54
]
, we also introduce a dictionary of functions with bounded support and prove an oracle inequality in Theorem
7.6
. This oracle inequality is stated under two conditions:
(i)
a control of the noise on each coordinate, to calibrate the LASSO, and
(ii)
a restricted eigenvalue property for the Gram matrix, which is a classical condition to obtain oracle inequalities
[
57
]
.

Calibration of the LASSO.
Following the method described in
[
28
,
54
]
, we derive data-dependent Bernstein-type inequalities for HAR processes to control the heterogeneous level of noise. In Theorem
7.9
we deduce weights that are measurable with respect to the data, and which fulfill with high probability condition
(i)
for the oracle inequality to hold.

Restricted eigenvalue for the Gram matrix.
For the oracle inequality to hold, the Gram matrix has to satisfy a Restricted Eigenvalue (RE) property (condition
(ii)
), see
[
57
]
. For the linear Hawkes processes, in
[
28
]
the authors proved that the Gram matrix is invertible with high probability, which is stronger than a RE property. However, in a high dimensional framework, requiring the Gram matrix to be invertible may be too strong and too restrictive. Thus, as in
[
54
]
we turn instead to a RE property. Another possible, and similar, method is to introduce so called
random compatibility constants
, see
[
57
]
, and
[
36
]
where this method is used in the framework of Hawkes estimation. However, neither
[
54
]
nor
[
36
]
prove, with high probability, that the RE property holds or that the random compatibility constants is positive. In
[
47
]
, the authors prove a RE property with high probability for discrete time Hawkes processes using Kalikow decomposition, but, up to our knowledge there is not any similar result for heterogeneous processes. For HAR processes, we prove that the RE property holds with high probability, see Theorem
7.14
. To achieve this, we use the coupling introduced in Section
6
in order to obtain concentration of the Gram matrix
G
around its expectation
\mathds{E}\left[G\right]
and finally, using a change of measure we link
\mathds{E}\left[G\right]
, which is not computable, to a quantity depending only on the chosen dictionary.

1.5
Outline

In Section
2
we rigorously define HAR processes and introduce the notations which will be used throughout the present article. Section
3
presents existence and stationary results. Linear HAR processes are introduced in Section
4
where we also provide the cluster representation. Exponential moments are derived in Section
5
. In Section
6
, we state the asymptotic limits, convergence to the stationary process and introduce the coupling and its properties leading to concentration inequalities. In Section
7
we present the statistical estimation of the interaction functions using the LASSO. The proofs of results of Sections
3
,
4
,
5
,
6
and
7
are given, respectively, in Sections
8
,
9
,
10
,
11
,
12
. Finally, some technical results are presented in Appendices
A
,
B
,
C
,
D
,
E
.

Notations.
We denote by
\mathds{R}
the set of real numbers and
\mathds{Z}
the set of the integers. By
\mathds{R}_{+}
we mean the non negative real numbers, ie
[0,\infty)
, and the non negative integers,
\{0,1,2\cdots\}
, are denoted by
\mathds{N}
. We denote
\mathds{N}^{*}
(resp.
\mathds{R}^{*}
,
\mathds{R}^{*}_{+}
, etc) the set
\mathds{N}\setminus\{0\}
(resp.
\mathds{R}\setminus\{0\}
,
\mathds{R}_{+}\setminus\{0\}
, etc).

2
Definition and Representation of HAR processes

Let
I\subset\mathds{R}
. A simple point process
S
on
I
is a random set of distinct points lying in
I
such that
\card(S\cap B)
is measurable for any borel subset
B
of
I
.
S
is said to be
non exploding
if almost surely
S
has only a finite number of points inside any bounded subset of
I
. The counting measure on
S
is denoted by
dS
. For
C\subset I
we denote by
S\cap C
the set of points lying in
C
and
S(C):=\card(S\cap C)
. A point process is fully determined by its counting measure or by the collection
(S(C))_{C\subset I}
. See
[
12
]
for more precise statements. In the sequel we will navigate freely between these different points of view.

Let
\bm{M}
a finite set. Consider a simple point process
S
on
I
with marks
Z\in\bm{M}
, which means that for all
x\in S
, the mark
Z(x)
is a random variable on
\bm{M}
. For
m\in\bm{M}
we can define
S^{m}=\{x\in S\mid Z(x)=m\}
.
S
is a
\bm{M}
-multivariate point process on
I
if
S^{m}
is a point process for all
m\in\bm{M}

[
12
]
. Note that by definition,
S^{m}
and
S^{m^{\prime}}
have no common points for
m\neq m^{\prime}
.

Let
M\in\mathds{N}
be the number of
nodes
and
P\in\mathds{N}
the number of
chains
. We denote by
\bm{M}:=[\![1,M]\!]
the set of nodes and
\bm{P}:=[\![-P,-1]\!]
the set of chains. Each chain
p\in\bm{P}
has a particular time embedding. More precisely the chain
p
is a random function
W^{p}:\mathcal{D}_{p}\longrightarrow\mathds{R}
where
\mathcal{D}_{p}:=\theta_{p}+\frac{1}{n_{p}}\mathds{Z}
. The positive integer
n_{p}\in\mathds{N}^{*}
is called the
frequency
of chain
p
and the value
\theta_{p}\in[0,1/n_{p})
is called the
phase
of chain
p
. We also denote by
{D}^{p}
the counting measure on
\mathcal{D}_{p}
and
\mathcal{Q}=\{(p,k),\ p\in\bm{P},\ k\in\mathcal{D}_{p}\}
.

Without loss of generality, we can suppose throughout the present article that the greatest common divisor of the frequencies
n_{p}
for
p\in\bm{P}
is 1, ie

\gcd(n_{p},\ p\in\bm{P})=1.

(2.1)

In this article, to construct the various processes on the same underlying probability space we use the formalism of
[
19
]
to represent Hawkes processes as the solution to a system of SDEs driven by Poisson random measures. Ultimately this representation will ease couplings between HAR processes.

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathds{P})
a probabilistic space with independent Poisson random measures
\pi^{m}
on
\mathds{R}\times\mathds{R}_{+}
for
m\in\bm{M}
. Suppose that we also have independent random real variables
\xi^{p}_{k}
for
(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q}
which are also independent of the Poisson random measures and such that for all
(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q}
, for all
n\in\mathds{Z}
, we have
\xi^{p}_{k}\sim\xi^{p}_{k+n}
in distribution. Since almost surely each atom of all the Poisson random measures have distinct time coordinates, for simplicity assume that it is
always
the case. We define a filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in\mathds{R}}
by

\mathcal{F}_{t}=\sigma\bigg(\pi^{m}\cap\big((-\infty,t]\times\mathds{R}_{+}\big),\ m\in\bm{M}\ \text{and}\ \xi^{p}_{k},\ (p,k)\in\mathcal{Q},\ k\leq t\bigg).

The collection of parameters associated to a HAR process is denoted
\mathfrak{M}
and takes the following form

\mathfrak{M}=\big((\mu_{m})_{m},\ (h^{\alpha}_{\beta})_{\alpha,\beta},\ (\Phi^{\alpha})_{\alpha},\ (\kappa^{\alpha}_{p})_{\alpha,p}\big)

where we have for
\alpha,\beta\in\bm{M}\cup\bm{P}
,
m\in\bm{M}
,
p\in\bm{P}

•

A function
h^{\alpha}_{\beta}:\mathds{R}_{+}^{*}\longrightarrow\mathds{R}
called
interaction function of
\alpha
on
\beta
.

•

A Lipschitz function
\kappa^{\alpha}_{p}:\mathds{R}\longrightarrow\mathds{R}
called
mask of chain
p
on
\alpha
.

•

A non negative non decreasing Lipschitz function
\Phi^{m}:\mathds{R}\longrightarrow\mathds{R}_{+}
called
link function of
m
.

•

A Lipschitz function
\Phi^{p}:\mathds{R}\longrightarrow\mathds{R}
such that
\Phi^{p}(0)=0
called
link function of
p
.

•

A real constant
\mu_{m}
called
base rate of node
m
.

As for AR processes, the chains of HAR processes are constructed with some noises, the
(\xi^{p}_{k})_{(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q}}
. Since we do not require them to be centred, we adopt the terminology
random drift
which is less misleading than noise.

Parameters
\mathfrak{M}
give birth to HAR processes
X=((\lambda^{m})_{m\in\bm{M}},\ (S^{m})_{m\in\bm{M}},\ (W^{p})_{p\in\bm{P}})
where the
(S^{m})_{m\in\bm{M}}
is a multivariate point process,
(\lambda^{m})_{m\in\bm{M}}
are the predictable stochastic intensities of the point process, and
(W^{p})_{p\in\bm{P}}
, the chains, are discrete processes defined on
(\mathcal{D}_{p})_{p\in\bm{P}}
. The process
X
is defined as the solution of equations with parameters given by
\mathfrak{M}
. One can ask for the equations to be satisfied on
\mathds{R}
or just after a time
t_{0}\in\mathds{R}
. In this second case, the process is initialised before
t_{0}
with an initial condition. An initial condition at time
t_{0}
is a random variable
\mathfrak{C}
which is
\mathcal{F}_{t_{0}-}
measurable and writes as follows

\mathfrak{C}=\Big(\big(\mathfrak{C}^{m}\big)_{m\in\bm{M}},\ \big(\mathfrak{C}^{p}_{k}\big)_{(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q},\ k<t_{0}}\Big),

where
\mathfrak{C}^{m}
is a subset of
(-\infty,t_{0})
that initialises the points of node
m
before
t_{0}
.
\mathfrak{C}^{p}_{k}
belongs to
\mathds{R}\cup\{\varnothing\}
and initialises the chain
p
at time
k
. If
\mathfrak{C}^{p}_{k}
takes the value
\varnothing
we use the convention that for any function
\kappa^{\alpha}_{\beta}
with
\alpha\in\bm{M}\cup\bm{P}
and
\beta\in\bm{P}
, we have
\kappa^{\alpha}_{\beta}(\varnothing)=0
.

Definition 2.1
.

Let some parameters
\mathfrak{M}
. We say that a process
X=\big((\lambda^{m})_{m\in\bm{M}},(S^{m})_{m\in\bm{M}},(W^{p})_{p\in\bm{P}}\big)
, adapted to
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t}
and with
(\lambda^{m})_{m\in\bm{M}}
predictable, is a
\bm{{\HAR}}_{\mathfrak{M}}\bm{(}-\infty\bm{)}
process, if we have

\displaystyle\lambda^{m}_{t}=\Phi^{m}\Bigg(\mu_{m}+\sum_{m^{\prime}\in\bm{M}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t-}h^{m}_{m^{\prime}}(t-s)dS^{m^{\prime}}_{s}+\sum_{p\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t-}h^{m}_{p}(t-k)\kappa^{m}_{p}(W^{p}_{k})d{D}^{p}_{k}\Bigg)

(2.2a)

\displaystyle S^{m}(C)=\displaystyle\int_{C\times\mathds{R}_{+}}\mathds{1}_{x\leq\lambda^{m}_{s}}d\pi^{m}(s,x)

(2.2b)

\displaystyle W^{p}_{k}=\xi^{p}_{k}+\Phi^{p}\Bigg(\sum_{m\in\bm{M}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{k-}h^{p}_{m}(k-s)dS^{m}_{s}+\sum_{p^{\prime}\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{k-}h^{p}_{p^{\prime}}(k-k^{\prime})\kappa^{p}_{p^{\prime}}(W^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}})d{D}^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}\Bigg),

(2.2c)

where (
2.2a
) holds
\mathds{P}(d\omega)\otimes dt
almost everywhere in
\Omega\times\mathds{R}
, (
2.2b
) holds almost surely for all
C\subset\mathds{R}
and (
2.2c
) holds almost surely for all
(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q}
. Such a process is called a HAR process with parameters
\mathfrak{M}
started at
-\infty
.

Let
t_{0}\in\mathds{R}
and an initial condition
\mathfrak{C}
at time
t_{0}
. We say that the process
X
, adapted to
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t}
and with
(\lambda^{m})_{m\in\bm{M}}
predictable, is a
\bm{{\HAR}}_{\mathfrak{M}}\bm{(}t_{0},\mathfrak{C}\bm{)}
process, if

1.

we have
S^{m}\cap(-\infty,t_{0})=\mathfrak{C}^{m}
for all
m\in\bm{M}
and
W^{p}_{k}=\mathfrak{C}^{p}_{k}
for all
(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q}
with
k<t_{0}
,

2.

(
2.2a
) holds
d\mathds{P}(\omega)\otimes dt
almost everywhere in
\Omega\times[t_{0},\infty)
, (
2.2b
) holds almost surely for all
C\subset[t_{0},\infty)
and (
2.2c
) holds almost surely for all
(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q}
with
k\geq t_{0}
.

Such a process is called a HAR process with parameters
\mathfrak{M}
started at
t_{0}
with initial condition
\mathfrak{C}
.

Some remarks are in order.

The realisation of point processes by SDEs driven by Poisson random measures is done in (
2.2b
). The intensity process,
\lambda^{m}_{t}
, is defined by (
2.2a
) and then
S^{m}
is defined to be atoms of the Poisson random measures lying below the intensity, which is the classical thinning for Hawkes processes
[
19
,
48
]
. As shown in Appendix
A
,
\lambda
is indeed the intensity of
S
, and, whenever
\lambda
is defined
d\mathds{P}\left(\omega\right)\otimes dt
almost everywhere,
S
is well defined.

We allow
\mathfrak{C}^{p}_{k}
to take the value
\varnothing
for the following reason. One may want to have a HAR process started at time
t_{0}
without initial condition, thus the logical choice is
\mathfrak{C}^{m}=\varnothing
and
\mathfrak{C}^{p}_{k}=0
. However, since the masks may have
\kappa^{\alpha}_{\beta}(0)\neq 0
, this choice of initial condition is not the desired one. Allowing the value
\varnothing
and forcing
\kappa^{\alpha}_{\beta}(\varnothing)=0
for any
\alpha,\beta
creates the desired initial condition.

For simplicity, we only write
\bm{{\HAR}}_{\mathfrak{M}}\bm{(}t_{0}\bm{)}
when the initial condition
\mathfrak{C}
is chosen to be the empty one (
\mathfrak{C}^{m}=\varnothing
for all
m
and
\mathfrak{C}^{p}_{k}=\varnothing
for all
(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q}
with
k<t_{0}
) and we denote this initial condition by
\mathfrak{C}^{\varnothing}
. In practice, it is equivalent to replace
-\infty
by
t_{0}
in the lower bounds of the integrals in (
2.2a
), (
2.2b
) and (
2.2c
).

Observe that if there are no chains, meaning
\bm{P}=\varnothing
, we obtain the classical Hawkes model. If there is no nodes, meaning
\bm{M}=\varnothing
, the chains satisfy the multivariate and multiscale version of (
1.3
).

The predictable intensity of the point process
S^{m}
associated to node
m
is
\lambda^{m}
. It satisfies (
2.2a
). Due to the function
\Phi^{m}
,
\lambda^{m}
is nonlinear with respect to the parameters of the model. Thus the HAR processes we consider are very general. The linear quantity

\ell^{m}_{t}:=\mu_{m}+\sum_{m^{\prime}\in\bm{M}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t-}h^{m}_{m^{\prime}}(t-s)dS^{m^{\prime}}_{s}+\sum_{p\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t-}h^{m}_{p}(t-k)\kappa^{m}_{p}(W^{p}_{k})d{D}^{p}_{k},

(2.3)

will be called
pre-intensity
in this article. It is defined so that
\lambda^{m}_{t}=\Phi^{m}(\ell^{m}_{t})
. It is made of three quantities being from left to right: a base rate (as in Hawkes processes), interactions from nodes on node
m
(as in Hawkes processes) and interactions from chains on node
m
. The pre-intensity
\ell^{m}
can take negative values, but since
\Phi^{m}\geq 0
the intensity
\lambda^{m}
is well defined.

If the parameters
\mu_{m},\ h^{m}_{m^{\prime}},\ h^{m}_{p},\ \kappa^{m}_{p}
for
m^{\prime}\in\bm{M}
and
p\in\bm{P}
are non negative, then we have
\ell^{m}_{t}\geq 0
. Thus, in this case one can take
\Phi^{m}=\max(0,\cdot)
and has
\lambda^{m}=\ell^{m}
. It is morally the same thing as taking
\Phi^{m}=\id
. And one obtains, if
\bm{P}=\varnothing
, the well known linear Hawkes process.

Chains satisfy (
2.2c
). As for classical AR processes, this equation is made of two elements: a possibly non centered noise
\xi^{p}_{k}
called random drift, and a term that depends on the past. The link function
\Phi^{p}
is required to satisfy
\Phi^{p}(0)=0
. It is not restrictive since a situation where
\Phi^{p}(0)\neq 0
fits our case by doing these modifications:
(\Phi^{p},\xi^{p})\leftarrow(\Phi^{p}-\Phi^{p}(0),\xi^{p}+\Phi^{p}(0))
. As for the equation of the intensity, the link function
\Phi^{p}
allows the general nonlinear case. Inside the link function, we have the interactions from the nodes on chain
p
and then the classical AR term of interactions from chains on chain
p
.

Let us introduce some notations.

The Lipschitz coefficients of the masks and the link functions are denoted as follows

a^{\alpha}_{p}:=\lip(\kappa^{\alpha}_{p}),\ L_{\alpha}:=\lip(\Phi^{\alpha})\quad p\in\bm{P},\ \alpha\in\bm{M}\cup\bm{P}.

(2.4)

For a function
f:\mathds{R}\longrightarrow\mathds{R}
and an positive real number
\alpha>0
we define the following norm which takes into account discrete behaviour at frequency scale
\alpha
,

\|f\|_{1,\alpha}^{\infty}:=\sup_{x\in\mathds{R}}\sum_{k\in\mathds{Z}}|f(x+k/\alpha)|.

In particular, if
f
is supported on
\mathds{R}_{+}^{*}
, then since
\mathcal{D}_{p}=\theta_{p}+\frac{1}{n_{p}}\mathds{Z}
we have

\|f\|_{1,n_{p}}^{\infty}=\sup_{t\in\mathds{R}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t-}|f(t-k)|d{D}^{p}_{k}.

The support of a function
f:I\longrightarrow\mathds{R}
with
I\subset\mathds{R}
, is denoted by
\supp(f)
.

A real function
f
is locally integrable, i.e.
f\in L^{1}_{loc}
, if for all compact sets
K\subset\mathds{R}
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{K}|f(x)|dx<\infty.

The identity matrix is denoted by
\Id_{n}
with
n
the dimension or
\Id
if there is no ambiguity. For a (complex valued) square matrix
N
the spectral radius of
N
, defined as the supremum of the magnitudes of the eigenvalues of
N
, is denoted as
\spr(N)
.

For
q\geq 1
, the
\ell^{q}
norm of the vector
x\in\mathds{R}^{n}
is denoted by
|x|_{q}
,

|x|_{q}=\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}|x_{i}|^{q}\Big)^{1/q}.

Given two vector
x
and
y
(or matrices) of same dimension, we use
\preceq
for the entry by entry comparison,

x\preceq y\ \iff\ \forall i,\ x_{i}\leq y_{i}.

Finally, for a vector
x
and a real function
f
, we denote by
f(x)
the coordinate wise application of
f
.

3
Existence results for HAR processes

Equations defining HAR processes are implicit, both the point processes and the chains depend on each others. Thus it is not clear that HAR processes exist. In this section we derive conditions to ensure existence of HAR processes. In case of Hawkes processes, it is well known whether or not there exist solutions to the equation defining Hawkes processes
[
11
,
2
]
. The main difference here is that the Hawkes process is mixed with discrete sequences driven by random drifts. For HAR processes started at a time
t_{0}\in\mathds{R}
with suitable initial condition (see Definition
3.2
), since the present value of the process depends only on the past we can construct the process on increasing intervals
[t_{0},t_{0}+t]
with
t\to\infty
, see Theorem
3.4
. However for HAR started at
t=-\infty
, see Theorem
3.9
, this constructive proof of existence is not relevant and we use instead a fixed point method similar to the one implemented in
[
11
]
.

3.1
Existence of non exploding HAR processes with initial condition

In this subsection we present the results on existence of HAR processes started at a finite time with suitable initial condition. We are only interested in processes that do not blow up to infinity in finite time: we need them to be non exploding, which means that the process is bounded on compact sets. This is explained by the following definition.

Definition 3.1

(Non exploding process)
.

A process
\big((\lambda^{m})_{m\in\bm{M}},(S^{m})_{m\in\bm{M}},(W^{p})_{p\in\bm{P}}\big)
is said to be non exploding if

1.

\forall m\in\bm{M},\ \forall C\subset\mathds{R}\ \text{bounded},\ S^{m}(C)<\infty
,

2.

\forall m\in\bm{M},\ \forall t\in\mathds{R},\ |\lambda^{m}_{t}|<\infty
,

3.

\forall p\in\bm{P},\ \forall k\in\mathcal{D}_{p},\ |W^{p}_{k}|<\infty
.

For a HAR process to be non exploding, one cannot allow arbitrary initial conditions. The following definition introduces
integrable
initial conditions.

Definition 3.2

(Integrable initial condition)
.

An initial condition
\mathfrak{C}
at time
t_{0}\in\mathds{R}
is said integrable if there exists a constant
K<\infty
such that

•

for all
m\in\bm{M}
, for all intervals
B\subset\mathds{R}
of length
1
,
\mathds{E}\left[\card(\mathfrak{C}^{m}\cap B)\right]\leq K
,

•

for all
(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q}
, we have
\mathds{E}\left[\mathds{1}_{\mathfrak{C}^{p}_{k}\neq\varnothing}|\mathfrak{C}^{p}_{k}|\right]\leq K
.

Since we are interested in existence and uniqueness results, we need to introduce the right notion of uniqueness for HAR processes.

Definition 3.3

(Modification)
.

Two processes
\big((\lambda^{m})_{m\in\bm{M}},(S^{m})_{m\in\bm{M}},(W^{p})_{p\in\bm{P}}\big)
and
\big((\tilde{\lambda}^{m})_{m\in\bm{M}},(\tilde{S}^{m})_{m\in\bm{M}},(\tilde{W}^{p})_{p\in\bm{P}}\big)
are said to be modifications of each others if

•

For all
m\in\bm{M}
,
\lambda^{m}_{t}=\tilde{\lambda}^{m}_{t}
almost everywhere in
\Omega\times\mathds{R}
for the measure
d\mathds{P}\left(\omega\right)\otimes dt
.

•

Almost surely, for all
m\in\bm{M}
,
S^{m}=\tilde{S}^{m}
.

•

Almost surely, for all
(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q}
,
W^{p}_{k}=\tilde{W}^{p}_{k}
.

Let us introduce the following assumption.

Assumption

(
NoExpl
)
.

Parameters
\mathfrak{M}
satisfy assumption
\bm{(}\text{{{NoExpl}}}\bm{)}
if for all
m\in\bm{M}
the link function
\Phi^{m}
is Lipschitz, i.e.
L_{m}<\infty
, and for all
m,m^{\prime}\in\bm{M}
, for all
p\in\bm{P}
the functions
h^{m}_{m^{\prime}},\ h^{m}_{p}
and
h^{p}_{m}
are in
L^{1}
.

We can now state the main result of the paragraph.

Theorem 3.4

(Existence of non exploding HAR processes)
.

Let some parameters
\mathfrak{M}
and an initial condition
\mathfrak{C}
at time
t_{0}
. Suppose that assumption
\bm{(}\text{{{NoExpl}}}\bm{)}
[\mathfrak{M}]
holds and that
\mathfrak{C}
is integrable. Then there exists a unique (up to modification)
\bm{{\HAR}}_{\mathfrak{M}}\bm{(}t_{0},\mathfrak{C}\bm{)}
process among the ones which are non exploding.

The assumptions of Theorem
3.4
are very mild and general and the initial condition can be taken as Poisson point processes, or even some kind of sufficiently evenly spaced deterministic points. For the chains, one can take iid random variables with finite expectation for example. However, if one wants to relax the
L^{1}
assumptions into only
L^{1}_{loc}
assumptions it is possible at the cost of other conditions on the initial condition, as explained in the following remark.

Remark 3.5
.

An initial condition
\mathfrak{C}
at time
t_{0}\in\mathds{R}
is said finite if

•

almost surely, for all
m\in\bm{M}
,
\card(\mathfrak{C}^{m})<\infty
,

•

almost surely, we have

\sup\ \{t<t_{0}\mid\forall(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q}\ \text{with}\ k\leq t,\ \mathfrak{C}^{p}_{k}=\varnothing\}>-\infty,

with the convention that
\sup(\varnothing)=-\infty
.

Finite initial conditions are not necessarily integrable and vice versa.

Assumption

(
NoExplBis
)
.

Parameters
\mathfrak{M}
satisfy assumption
\bm{(}\text{{{NoExplBis}}}\bm{)}
if for all
m\in\bm{M}
the link function
\Phi^{m}
is Lipschitz, ie
L_{m}<\infty
, and for all
m,m^{\prime}\in\bm{M}
, for all
p\in\bm{P}
the functions
h^{m}_{m^{\prime}},\ h^{m}_{p}
are in
L^{1}_{loc}
.

Theorem
3.4
applies to finite initial conditions under assumption
\bm{(}\text{{{NoExplBis}}}\bm{)}
[\mathfrak{M}]
. The proof is almost exactly the same as explained in Remark
8.1
. Assumption
\bm{(}\text{{{NoExplBis}}}\bm{)}
[\mathfrak{M}]
matches the classical criterion of non explosion of linear Hawkes processes with empty initial condition: the interacting functions are
L^{1}_{loc}
, see
[
2
]
. This criterion naturally extends to nonlinear Hawkes process by assuming in addition that the link functions, here the
\Phi^{m}
, are Lipschitz.

In the sequel, we keep assumption
\bm{(}\text{{{NoExpl}}}\bm{)}
and integrable initial conditions.

3.2
Existence of stationary HAR processes

Establishing existence of stationary processes is crucial in particular for practical purposes, for example in statistical applications
[
28
]
, or because they appear as long time limits of non stationary processes
[
11
]
. In general, HAR processes started at a finite time are not stationary. This motivates to look at HAR processes started at
-\infty
. Since HAR processes are a mix between discrete and continuous processes, we need to define what stationary means for HAR processes.

Definition 3.6

(1-stationary)
.

A process
X=\Big(\big[(\lambda^{m}_{t})_{t\in\mathds{R}},\ m\in\bm{M}\big],\big[S^{m},\ m\in\bm{M}\big],\big[(W^{p}_{k})_{k\in\mathcal{D}_{p}},\ p\in\bm{P}\big]\Big)
is said to be 1-stationary if

X\overset{\text{law}}{=}\Big(\big[(\lambda^{m}_{t+1})_{t\in\mathds{R}},\ m\in\bm{M}\big],\big[S^{m}+1,\ m\in\bm{M}\big],\big[(W^{p}_{k+1})_{k\in\mathcal{D}_{p}},\ p\in\bm{P}\big]\Big)

Remark 3.7
.

Stationarity is defined with a period equal to 1. We cannot decrease the period since we assume that the greatest common divisor of the
n_{p}
for
p\in\bm{P}
is
1
, see (
2.1
). Thus the family of time indexes of the chains, meaning
(\mathcal{D}_{p})_{p\in\bm{P}}
, is only 1-periodic:
(\mathcal{D}_{p})_{p\in\bm{P}}=(\mathcal{D}_{p}+1)_{p\in\bm{P}}
.

The classical condition to prove the existence of a stationary Hawkes process is based on the spectral radius of a matrix which captures the interactions between nodes. More precisely, the spectral radius of the matrix containing the
L^{1}
norms of the interaction functions has to be strictly smaller than one
[
11
]
. Our condition will be of the same kind. The following definition introduces the matrix of the norms of the interactions between both nodes and chains on which the major assumption to the existence of 1-stationary HAR processes relies.

Definition 3.8
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}
some parameters. Recall that for
m\in\bm{M},\ p,p^{\prime}\in\bm{P}

a^{p}_{p^{\prime}}=\lip(\kappa^{p}_{p^{\prime}}),\ a^{m}_{p}=\lip(\kappa^{m}_{p}),\ L_{m}=\lip(\Phi^{m}),\ L_{p}=\lip(\Phi^{p}).

We introduce the following matrices:

H^{S}_{S}=(L_{m}\|h^{m}_{m^{\prime}}\|_{1})_{m,m^{\prime}\in\bm{M}},\ H^{S}_{W}=(L_{m}a^{m}_{p}\|h^{m}_{p}\|_{1,n_{p}}^{\infty})_{m\in\bm{M},p\in\bm{P}},\ H^{W}_{S}=(L_{p}\|h^{p}_{m}\|_{1})_{p\in\bm{P},m\in\bm{M}}

\text{and}\ H^{W}_{W}=(L_{p}a^{p}_{p^{\prime}}\|h^{p}_{p^{\prime}}\|_{1,n_{p^{\prime}}}^{\infty})_{p,p^{\prime}\in\bm{P}}.

Finally, the matrix
H_{\mathfrak{M}}
is defined by

H_{\mathfrak{M}}=\begin{pmatrix}H^{S}_{S}&H^{S}_{W}\\[4.0pt]
H^{W}_{S}&H^{W}_{W}\end{pmatrix}.

Infinite values are allowed with the convention that
\infty\times 0=0\times\infty=\infty
.

Let us introduce some assumptions before stating the theorem about 1-stationary HAR processes.

Assumption

(
Spec1
)
.

We say that parameters
\mathfrak{M}
satisfy assumption
\bm{(}\text{{{Spec1}}}\bm{)}
if the spectral radius of the matrix
H_{\mathfrak{M}}
introduced in Definition
3.8
is smaller than one, equivalently
\spr(H_{\mathfrak{M}})<1
.

Assumption

(
FiMe
)
.

The random drifts have finite mean, equivalently,

\sup_{(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathds{E}\left[|\xi^{p}_{k}|\right]<\infty.

Now, we can state the main result concerning existence of HAR processes started at
-\infty
. It also answers questions about uniqueness and 1-stationary properties.

Theorem 3.9

(Stationary HAR processes)
.

Let some parameters
\mathfrak{M}
. Suppose that assumptions
\bm{(}\text{{{Spec1}}}\bm{)}
[\mathfrak{M}]
and
\bm{(}\text{{{FiMe}}}\bm{)}
hold. Then, up to modification, there exists a unique
\bm{{\HAR}}_{\mathfrak{M}}\bm{(}-\infty\bm{)}
process
((\lambda^{m})_{m\in\bm{M}},\ (S^{m})_{m\in\bm{M}},\ (W^{p})_{p\in\bm{P}})
such that

\sup_{m\in\bm{M},\ t\in\mathds{R}}\mathds{E}\left[|\lambda^{m}_{t}|\right]<\infty\ \text{and}\ \sup_{p\in\bm{P},\ k\in\mathcal{D}_{p}}\mathds{E}\left[|W^{p}_{k}|\right]<\infty.

Moreover, this process is also 1-stationary.

For classical Hawkes processes, Brémaud and Massoulié in
[
11
]
have proved that a stationary version exists if the spectral radius of the matrix gathering the
L^{1}
norms of the interaction functions is smaller than one. Assumption
\bm{(}\text{{{Spec1}}}\bm{)}
[\mathfrak{M}]
naturally extends this condition. Assumption
\bm{(}\text{{{FiMe}}}\bm{)}
is a very mild assumption on the random drifts. In addition of providing an existence result, Theorem
3.9
also shows uniqueness among processes bounded in
L^{1}
. Finally, it validates the intuition that a process started at
-\infty
is stationary since it has already evolved during an infinite period of time.

4
Linear HAR processes and cluster representation

In the previous section we established existence results, to continue our study we aim for a specific class of HAR processes on which a lot of calculations are possible. To draw a parallel, in the study of Hawkes processes, linear Hawkes processes play a major role since they allow lot of calculations due to their famous cluster representation
[
29
]
. Cluster representation allows to quantify exactly and isolate the impact of each point in the future. We will be able to derive a similar cluster representation for HAR processes with
linear
parameters. We define the
\mathfrak{L}
class, the class of linear parameters, as follows.

Definition 4.1

(
\mathfrak{L}
class)
.

A set a parameters
\mathfrak{M}
is said to be linear, or equivalently
\mathfrak{M}\in\mathfrak{L}
if

•

(linearity 1)
\forall m\in\bm{M}
,
\Phi^{m}=\id
and
\forall p\in\bm{P}
,
\Phi^{p}=\id
,

•

(linearity 2)
\forall\alpha\in\bm{M}\cup\bm{P}
,
\forall p\in\bm{P}
we have
\kappa^{\alpha}_{p}(x)=a^{\alpha}_{p}x+b^{\alpha}_{p}
for any
x\in\mathds{R}
and
\kappa^{\alpha}_{p}(\varnothing)=0
, with
a^{\alpha}_{p},b^{\alpha}_{p}
non negative constants,

•

(positiveness)
\forall\alpha,\beta\in\bm{M}\cup\bm{P}
, we have
h^{\alpha}_{\beta}\geq 0
and
\forall m\in\bm{M},\ \mu_{m}\geq 0
.

Remark 4.2
.

To ensure that the intensity remains positive, if there is an initial condition it has to be non negative (ie
\mathfrak{C}^{p}_{k}\geq 0
or
\mathfrak{C}^{p}_{k}=\varnothing
). The random drifts
\xi^{p}_{k}
also have to be non negative. The definition of
\kappa^{\alpha}_{p}
remains coherent with
\lip(\kappa^{\alpha}_{p})=a^{\alpha}_{p}
.

There are two major linearity assumptions defining the
\mathfrak{L}
class, a first one on the link functions and a second one on the masks. Positiveness assumption, in addition with non negative random drifts
\xi^{p}_{k}
, is here to ensure that in the end the intensity is non negative. Recall the equation of the chains (
2.2c
),

W^{p}_{k}=\xi^{p}_{k}+\Phi^{p}\Bigg(\sum_{m\in\bm{M}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{k-}h^{p}_{m}(k-s)dS^{m}_{s}+\sum_{p^{\prime}\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{k-}h^{p}_{p^{\prime}}(k-k^{\prime})\kappa^{p}_{p^{\prime}}(W^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}})d{D}^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}\Bigg).

Under the assumptions on the masks and the link functions, this equation becomes a linear relation on the past values of chains. One has, heuristically and in the one dimensional case,

\displaystyle W_{k}

\displaystyle=\xi_{k}+(\text{points linear contribution up to}\ k)+(\text{non random term})+\sum_{k^{\prime}<k}ah(k-k^{\prime})W_{k^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=(\text{something that depends on k})+\sum_{k^{\prime}<k}ah(k-k^{\prime})W_{k^{\prime}}.

This recurrence relation is the starting point to derive the cluster representation for HAR processes. By solving it completely we will be able to express the chains only in terms of linear functions of points and random drifts. Then introducing this expression of the chains in the intensity equation will lead us to the cluster representation. In the next subsections we solve completely this type of recurrence relation to then be able to apply these results to HAR processes.

4.1
Recurrence relation of chains

The main assumptions in the
\mathfrak{L}
class are (linearity 1) and (linearity 2) on the link functions and the masks. In the general case each chain depends on the previous ones through a complex recurrence relationship. What makes these two assumptions interesting is that the complex dependence of a chain on the previous ones becomes a linear dependence! Let us give a simple example. Let a sequence
(w_{n})_{n\geq 0}
defined as follows

w_{n}=u_{n}+\sum_{0\leq k<n}h(n-k)w_{k}

where the sequence
u
is given and
h
is a fixed (say non negative) function. A natural wish is to have an expression of
w_{n}
depending only on the sequence
u
. To do so, we need to unroll the recurrence relationship. In the end, it is easy to be convinced that
w_{n}
can be expressed as follows

w_{n}=\sum_{0\leq k\leq n}C^{n}_{k}u_{k}

with
(C^{n}_{k})_{n\geq k}
be some coefficients depending only on
h
, see classical AR theory
[
10
,
53
,
42
]
. These coefficients grasp the complexity of the nested dependence of the sequence
w
on itself. One can prove for instance that these coefficients satisfy

•

For
k>k^{\prime}
we have

C^{k}_{k^{\prime}}=\sum_{k^{\prime}\leq k^{\prime\prime}<k}h(k-k^{\prime\prime})C^{k^{\prime\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}

•

For all
k\geq k^{\prime}
,
C^{k+1}_{k^{\prime}+1}=C^{k}_{k^{\prime}}

•

If
\sum_{k\geq 0}h(k)=:\alpha<1
then

\sum_{k=-\infty}^{n}C_{k}^{n}<\dfrac{1}{1-\alpha}.

We extend these results to the multidimensional case (since we have
P
chains) and on a domain
(\mathcal{D}_{p})_{p\in\bm{P}}
, more complex than the integers.

The following lemma introduces the coefficients
\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{p,k}{p',k'}_{\!\!\mathfrak{M}}
which are the analog’s of the
C^{k}_{k^{\prime}}
’s in the multidimensional case.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let parameters
\mathfrak{M}\in\mathfrak{L}
. There exists a unique set of coefficients
\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{p,k}{p',k'}_{\!\!\mathfrak{M}}\right)_{(p,k),(p^{\prime},k^{\prime})\in\mathcal{Q},\ k\geq k^{\prime}}
such that,

1.

For any
(p_{0},k_{0})\in\mathcal{Q}
, for any sequence
(Z^{p}_{k})_{(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q},\ k\geq k_{0}}
, the sequence
(V^{p}_{k})_{(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q},\ k\geq k_{0}}
defined by

V^{p}_{k}=Z^{p}_{k}+\sum_{p^{\prime}\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{k_{0}}^{k-}a^{p}_{p^{\prime}}h^{p}_{p^{\prime}}(k-k^{\prime})V^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}d{D}^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}

(4.1)

satisfies the following equality,

V^{p}_{k}=\sum_{p^{\prime}\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{k_{0}}^{k}\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{p,k}{p',k'}_{\!\!\mathfrak{M}}Z^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}d{D}^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}.

2.

The coefficients
\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{p,k}{p',k'}_{\!\!\mathfrak{M}}\right)_{(p,k),(p^{\prime},k^{\prime})\in\mathcal{Q},\ k\geq k^{\prime}}
are 1-periodic, which means that for every
n\in\mathds{Z}
,

\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{p,k+n}{p',k'+n}_{\!\!\mathfrak{M}}=\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{p,k}{p',k'}_{\!\!\mathfrak{M}}.

3.

For all
(p_{0},k_{0})\in\mathcal{Q}
we have
\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{p_0,k_0}{p_0,k_0}_{\!\!\mathfrak{M}}=1
. If there exists
(p_{1},k_{1})\in\mathcal{Q}
with
k_{1}=k_{0}
then
\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{p_1,k_0}{p_0,k_0}_{\!\!\mathfrak{M}}=\mathds{1}_{p_{0}=p_{1}}
. Finally for
(p^{\prime},k^{\prime})\in\mathcal{Q}
with
k>k^{\prime}
,

\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{p,k}{p',k'}_{\!\!\mathfrak{M}}=\sum_{p^{\prime\prime}\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{k^{\prime}}^{k-}a^{p}_{p^{\prime\prime}}h^{p}_{p^{\prime\prime}}(k-k^{\prime\prime})\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{p'',k''}{p',k'}_{\!\!\mathfrak{M}}d{D}^{p^{\prime\prime}}_{k^{\prime\prime}}\geq 0.

Lemma
4.3
is about the combinatorial properties of the coefficients. The coefficients only depend on
\mathfrak{M}
, they can be computed recursively with the third point of Lemma
4.3
. They are encoding all the dynamic of the recurrence relation defined by the functions
a^{p}_{p^{\prime}}h^{p}_{p^{\prime}}(\cdot)
.

Recall from Definition
3.8
that
H^{W}_{W}
is the matrix
(L_{p}a^{p}_{p^{\prime}}\|h^{p}_{p^{\prime}}\|_{1,n_{p^{\prime}}}^{\infty})_{p,p^{\prime}\in\bm{P}}
. We recall that for linear parameters
L_{p}=1
. Let the following assumption.

Assumption

(
Spec1.1
)
.

Wz say that parameters
\mathfrak{M}
satisfy assumption
\bm{(}\text{{{Spec1.1}}}\bm{)}
if the spectral radius of the matrix
H_{W}^{W}
defined in Definition
3.8
is smaller than one, equivalently
\spr(H^{W}_{W})<1
.

Under assumption
\bm{(}\text{{{Spec1.1}}}\bm{)}
we obtain analytic results on the coefficients, see Lemma
B.1
in the Appendix
B
. These results are useful in the sequel, for example to obtain controls on clusters.

We end this subsection with the following result that gives a closed form for the coefficients.

Let
(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q}
and
(p^{\prime},k^{\prime})\in\mathcal{Q}
with
k^{\prime}<k
. A path
\gamma
from
(p^{\prime},k^{\prime})
to
(p,k)
is a sequence
(p_{1},k_{1}),\cdots,(p_{n},k_{n})
in
\mathcal{Q}
, where
n\geq 2
is an integer and such that
(p_{1},k_{1})=(p^{\prime},k^{\prime})
,
(p_{n},k_{n})=(p,k)
and
k_{1}<\cdots<k_{n}
. Given parameters
\mathfrak{M}\in\mathfrak{L}
and a path
\gamma=(p_{1},k_{1}),\cdots,(p_{n},k_{n})
from
(p^{\prime},k^{\prime})
to
(p,k)
we denote

Y_{\mathfrak{M}}(\gamma):=\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}a^{p_{i+1}}_{p_{i}}h^{p_{i+1}}_{p_{i}}(k_{i+1}-k_{i}).

Proposition 4.4

(Closed form for coefficients)
.

Let some parameters
\mathfrak{M}\in\mathfrak{L}
. Let
(p^{\prime},k^{\prime})\in\mathcal{Q}
and
(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q}
with
k^{\prime}<k
. Then we have

\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{p,k}{p',k'}_{\!\!\mathfrak{M}}=\sum_{\gamma:(p^{\prime},k^{\prime})\to(p,k)}Y_{\mathfrak{M}}(\gamma).

4.2
Cluster representation for linear HAR processes

In this subsection we use the coefficients defined in the previous subsection to unroll the nested dynamic of chains and reveal the cluster representation for linear HAR processes. Let parameters
\mathfrak{M}\in\mathfrak{L}
and
t_{0}\in\{-\infty\}\cup\mathds{R}
. We state the result for HAR processes without initial condition. As explain in Remark
4.6
, a similar result can be written for HAR processes with initial condition with the exact same ideas up to more complex writing.

Let us define quantities that are useful to express the chains in a non nested way. Let
(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q}
with
k\geq t_{0}
and define,

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\Hd^{b}_{p,k}(t_{0})=\sum_{p^{\prime}\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{k}\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{p,k}{p',k'}_{\!\!\mathfrak{M}}\left[\sum_{p^{\prime\prime}\in\bm{P}}b^{p^{\prime}}_{p^{\prime\prime}}\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{k^{\prime}-}h^{p^{\prime}}_{p^{\prime\prime}}(k^{\prime}-k^{\prime\prime})d{D}^{p^{\prime\prime}}_{k^{\prime\prime}}\right]d{D}^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}\\
&\Hd^{\xi}_{p,k}(t_{0})=\sum_{p^{\prime}\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{k}\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{p,k}{p',k'}_{\!\!\mathfrak{M}}\xi^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}d{D}^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}\\
&\Hd^{m}_{p,k}(t_{0},t)=\sum_{p^{\prime}\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{k}\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{p,k}{p',k'}_{\!\!\mathfrak{M}}\mathds{1}_{t<k^{\prime}}h^{p^{\prime}}_{m}(k^{\prime}-t)d{D}^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}.\end{aligned}\right.

(4.2)

Each of these quantities encloses the total impact at time
k
on chain
p
of, from up to down, the constants
b
’s of the masks, the random drifts, node
m
. If
t_{0}=-\infty
we may omit
t_{0}
in the notation an only write only
\Hd^{b}_{p,k}
and so on.

The following result is the main theorem of this section, it states a cluster representation for linear models.

Theorem 4.5

(Cluster representation for linear HAR processes)
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}\in\mathfrak{L}
. If
t_{0}\in\mathds{R}
suppose that assumption
\bm{(}\text{{{NoExpl}}}\bm{)}
[\mathfrak{M}]
holds and if
t_{0}=-\infty
suppose that assumptions
\bm{(}\text{{{Spec1}}}\bm{)}
[\mathfrak{M}]
and
\bm{(}\text{{{FiMe}}}\bm{)}
hold. Suppose also that the random drifts are non negative, ie
\xi^{p}_{k}\geq 0
for all
(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q}
. Then the
\bm{{\HAR}}_{\mathfrak{M}}\bm{(}t_{0}\bm{)}
process from Theorem
3.4
(if
t_{0}\in\mathds{R}
) or Theorem
3.9
(if
t_{0}=-\infty
) must be given by

W^{p}_{k}=\Hd^{b}_{p,k}(t_{0})+\Hd^{\xi}_{p,k}(t_{0})+\sum_{m}\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{k-}\Hd^{m}_{p,k}(t_{0},t)dS^{m}_{t},\quad(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q},\ k\geq t_{0},

and there exists a cluster representation given by

\displaystyle\lambda^{m}_{t}=\mathcal{I}^{m}(t_{0},t)+\sum_{m^{\prime}}\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{t-}\mathfrak{h}^{m}_{m^{\prime}}(s,t)dS^{m^{\prime}}_{s},\quad m\in\bm{M},\ t\geq t_{0},

where any point, say from node
m_{0}
at time
s\in\mathds{R}
, has an impact on the intensity at time
t>s
of node
m
being

\mathfrak{h}^{m}_{m_{0}}(s,t)=h^{m}_{m_{0}}(t-s)+\sum_{p,p^{\prime}\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{s^{+}}^{t-}\displaystyle\int_{s^{+}}^{k}a^{m}_{p}h^{m}_{p}(t-k)\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{p,k}{p',k'}_{\!\!\mathfrak{M}}h^{p^{\prime}}_{m_{0}}(k^{\prime}-s)d{D}^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}d{D}^{p}_{k},

(4.3)

and the immigrant rate is given by

\mathcal{I}^{m}(t_{0},t)=\mu_{m}+\sum_{p\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{t-}\left[h^{m}_{p}(t-k)\times(a^{m}_{p}\Hd^{b}_{p,k}(t_{0})+a^{m}_{p}\Hd^{\xi}_{p,k}(t_{0})+b^{m}_{p})\right]d{D}^{p}_{k}.

(4.4)

Finally if assumption
\bm{(}\text{{{Spec1.1}}}\bm{)}
[\mathfrak{M}]
holds (which is the case under assumption
\bm{(}\text{{{Spec1}}}\bm{)}
[\mathfrak{M}]
),

\|\mathfrak{h}^{m}_{m_{0}}(s,\cdot)\|_{1,(s,\infty)}\leq\|h^{m}_{m_{0}}\|_{1}+\sum_{p,p^{\prime}\in\bm{P}}n_{p}a^{m}_{p}\|h^{m}_{p}\|_{1}\Big[(\Id-H^{W}_{W})^{-1}\Big]_{p,p^{\prime}}\|h^{p^{\prime}}_{m_{0}}\|_{1,1}^{\infty}.

(4.5)

Each immigrant point (coming from the immigration part of the intensity) is a new root for a new cluster. Indeed, each immigrant point, say from node
m
at time
s
, will have some children since it impacts the future intensity of node
m^{\prime}
at time
s+t
by
\mathfrak{h}^{m^{\prime}}_{m}(s,s+t)
. Similarly, each child will have children and so on, creating a cluster, ie the whole family of the initial immigrant point. Conditionally to the immigrants, all the clusters are independent. See Figure
1
. Cluster representation is a powerful tool, to derive result on the whole process; one can look at what happens for a single cluster and then the it only remains to superpose the contributions of each clusters, which is usually fine since the clusters are independent.

time

Fig 1:
Illustration of the clusters. The red dots are the immigrant points. They arrive with the immigrant rate and give birth to clusters, represented by the blue shapes.

Remark 4.6
.

Let
t_{0}\in\mathds{R}
and
\mathfrak{C}
a non negative integrable initial condition at time
t_{0}
, which means that
\mathfrak{C}^{p}_{k}\geq 0
or
\mathfrak{C}^{p}_{k}=\varnothing
for all
(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q}
with
k<t_{0}
. Let

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\Jd^{m,\mathfrak{C}}_{t}(t_{0})=\sum_{m^{\prime}\in\bm{M}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t_{0}-}h^{m}_{m^{\prime}}(t-s)d\mathfrak{C}^{m^{\prime}}_{s}+\sum_{p\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t_{0}-}h^{m}_{p}(t-k)\kappa^{m}_{p}(\mathfrak{C}^{p}_{k})d{D}^{p}_{k}\\
&\Jd^{p,\mathfrak{C}}_{k}(t_{0})=\sum_{m\in\bm{M}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t_{0}-}h^{p}_{m}(k-s)d\mathfrak{C}^{m}_{s}+\sum_{p^{\prime}\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t_{0}-}h^{p}_{p^{\prime}}(k-k^{\prime})\kappa^{m}_{p}(\mathfrak{C}^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}})d{D}^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}\\
&\Hd^{\mathfrak{C}}_{p,k}(t_{0})=\sum_{p^{\prime}\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{k}\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{p,k}{p',k'}_{\!\!\mathfrak{M}}\Jd^{p^{\prime},\mathfrak{C}}_{k^{\prime}}(t_{0})d{D}^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}\end{aligned}\right.

(4.6)

\Jd^{m,\mathfrak{C}}_{t}(t_{0})
and
\Jd^{p,\mathfrak{C}}_{k}(t_{0})
correspond, respectively, to the terms in (
2.2a
) and (
2.2c
) coming from the initial condition.
\Hd^{\mathfrak{C}}_{p,k}(t_{0})
correspond to the resulting term coming from the initial condition in
W^{p}_{k}
after solving the nested recurrence relation of the chains. Then, if
\mathfrak{C}
is integrable, under the assumptions of Theorem
4.5
, up to the following underlined changes, the result of Theorem
4.5
is the same;

\displaystyle W^{p}_{k}=\underline{\Hd^{\mathfrak{C}}_{p,k}(t_{0})}+\Hd^{b}_{p,k}(t_{0})+\Hd^{\xi}_{p,k}(t_{0})+\sum_{m}\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{k-}\Hd^{m}_{p,k}(t_{0},t)dS^{m}_{t},

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{m}(t_{0},t)=\mu_{m}+\underline{\Jd^{m,\mathfrak{C}}_{t}(t_{0})}+\sum_{p\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{t-}\left[h^{m}_{p}(t-k)\times(\underline{a^{m}_{p}\Hd^{\mathfrak{C}}_{p,k}(t_{0})}+a^{m}_{p}\Hd^{b}_{p,k}(t_{0})+a^{m}_{p}\Hd^{\xi}_{p,k}(t_{0})+b^{m}_{p})\right]d{D}^{p}_{k}.

4.3
The structure of clusters

In this subsection we dive into the study of clusters. The following definition recalls the expression of the functions generating clusters and introduces a relevant matrix.

Definition 4.7

(Cluster functions and matrix)
.

Let parameters
\mathfrak{M}\in\mathfrak{L}
. The cluster functions associated to linear parameters
\mathfrak{M}
are the functions
\mathfrak{h}^{m}_{m^{\prime}}
for
m,m^{\prime}\in\bm{M}
defined in Theorem
4.5
by, for
s<t
,

\mathfrak{h}^{m}_{m^{\prime}}(s,t)=h^{m}_{m^{\prime}}(t-s)+\sum_{p,p^{\prime}\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{s+}^{t-}\displaystyle\int_{s+}^{k}a^{m}_{p}h^{m}_{p}(t-k)\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{p,k}{p',k'}_{\!\!\mathfrak{M}}h^{p^{\prime}}_{m^{\prime}}(k^{\prime}-s)d{D}^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}d{D}^{p}_{k}.

For
\mathfrak{M}\in\mathfrak{L}
, the matrix
\mathfrak{H}_{\mathfrak{M}}
is defined by

\mathfrak{H}_{\mathfrak{M}}=\left(\sup_{s\in\mathds{R}}\|\mathfrak{h}^{m}_{m^{\prime}}(s,\cdot)\|_{1,(s,\infty)}\right)_{m,m^{\prime}\in\bm{M}}.

(4.7)

Let parameters
\mathfrak{M}\in\mathfrak{L}
, we are interested in the clusters generated by the cluster functions
\mathfrak{h}^{m}_{m^{\prime}}
defined in Definition
4.7
. As we will see later, the matrix
(\mathfrak{H}_{\mathfrak{M}})^{\intercal}
, defined in Definition
4.7
, gives a uniform bound on the expectation of the offspring distribution of clusters. This section follows
[
41
]
.

Let

\bm{\mathfrak{h}}=(\mathfrak{h}^{m}_{m^{\prime}})_{m,m^{\prime}\in\bm{M}}.

Clusters for linear HAR processes correspond to
{P}\!{o}\!{i}\!{s}(\bm{\mathfrak{h}}^{\intercal})
clusters in
[
41
]
. We explain below what it means, but for further details we refer the reader to
[
41
]
.

Remark 4.8
.

The relevant parameter is
\bm{\mathfrak{h}}^{\intercal}
and not
\bm{\mathfrak{h}}
since
\mathfrak{h}^{m}_{m^{\prime}}
corresponds to how many (in expectation) children of type
m
a point from node
m^{\prime}
will have, whereas, in the proof of Theorem
3.9
for example,
\mathfrak{h}^{m}_{m^{\prime}}
corresponds to how much previous points of node
m^{\prime}
modify the intensity of node
m
. This small change of perspective, backward or forward, is the explanation on why
\bm{\mathfrak{h}}^{\intercal}
and
(\mathfrak{H}_{\mathfrak{M}})^{\intercal}
are the relevant quantities for clusters.

We use the Ulam Harris Neveu notation to label the individuals of trees, see (
2
), and thus a tree is viewed as a subset of
\mathcal{U}=\cup_{n\in\mathds{N}}(\mathds{N}^{*})^{n}
with
(\mathds{N}^{*})^{0}=\{\varnothing\}
.

Given parameters
\mathfrak{M}\in\mathfrak{L}
, a cluster
G^{m_{0}}_{t_{0}}
with root of type
m_{0}\in\bm{M}
and born at time
t_{0}\in\mathds{R}
is defined by
\big((u,\tp(u),\bd(u))\big)_{u\in\mathcal{T}}
where

•

\mathcal{T}\subset\mathcal{U}
is a random tree. For
u\in\mathcal{T}
,
\tp(u)\in\bm{M}
is the type of
u
and
\bd(u)\in\mathds{R}
is the birth date of
u
.

•

\mathcal{T}
has root
\varnothing
of type
m_{0}
(ie
\tp(\varnothing)=m_{0}
) and born at time
t_{0}
(ie
\bd(\varnothing)=t_{0}
).

•

Independently each individual
u\in\mathcal{T}
reproduces as follows. If we denote
X(u,m)
the number of children of
u
with type
m\in\bm{M}
, then for
m\in\bm{M}
the random variables
X(u,m)
are independent and

X(u,m)\sim{P}\!{o}\!{i}\!{s}\big(\|\mathfrak{h}^{m}_{\tp(u)}(\bd(u),\bd(u)+\cdot)\|_{1,(0,\infty)}\big)\quad\forall m\in\bm{M}.

The birth dates of the children of
u
are distributed as follows. Conditionally to the children of
u
and their types, independently for any child
v\in\mathcal{T}
of
u
, we have

\bd(v)\sim\frac{\mathfrak{h}^{\tp(v)}_{\tp(u)}(\bd(u),t)}{\|\mathfrak{h}^{\tp(v)}_{\tp(u)}(\bd(u),\bd(u)+\cdot)\|_{1,(0,\infty)}}\mathds{1}_{t>\bd(u)}dt.

One may remark that
\mathfrak{h}^{m}{\tp(v)}_{\tp(u)}=\big(\bm{\mathfrak{h}}^{\intercal}\big)_{\tp(u),\tp(v)}
, thus, as in
[
41
]
, row indexes correspond to father/mother and column indexes correspond to children.

Finally, if one omits the birth dates of a cluster
G
, it remains
(u,\tp(u))_{u\in\mathcal{T}}
. It is clear that
(u,\tp(u))_{u\in\mathcal{T}}
can be stochastically dominated by
(u,\tp_{\mathcal{A}}(u))_{u\in\mathcal{A}}
(which means that
\mathcal{T}\subset\mathcal{A}
and for
u\in\mathcal{T}
, we have
\tp(u)=\tp_{\mathcal{A}}(u)
) where
(u,\tp_{\mathcal{A}}(u))_{u\in\mathcal{A}}
is a
{P}\!{o}\!{i}\!{s}(\mathfrak{H_{\mathfrak{M}}}^{\intercal})
Galton Watson process. It means that independently, any point
u\in\mathcal{A}
with type (in
\mathcal{A}
)
\tp_{\mathcal{A}}(u)
reproduces as follows: independently for all
m\in\bm{M}
it has
{P}\!{o}\!{i}\!{s}\big(\big[\mathfrak{H}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\intercal}\big]_{\tp_{\mathcal{A}}(u),m}\big)
children with type
m
.

(u=\varnothing,\tp(\varnothing)=m_{0},\bd(\varnothing)=t_{0})
u=0
u=1
00
10
11
12
(u,\tp(u),\bd(u))

Fig 2:
Illustration of a cluster. The tree
\mathcal{T}
is represented with the types and birth dates. The ancestor is
u=\varnothing
, it has two children,
u=0
and
u=1
.
u=0
has one child (
u=00
),
u=1
has three children (
u=10
,
u=11
and
u=12
) and so on.

Given this precise structure of clusters, the cluster representation takes the following form.

Proposition 4.9

(Cluster representation)
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}\in\mathfrak{L}
some parameters. Suppose that we are under the same assumptions as Theorem
4.5
. Let
(\Pi^{m})_{m\in\bm{M}}
be point processes such that, conditionally to
(\xi^{p}_{k})_{(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q}}
,
(\Pi^{m})_{m\in\bm{M}}
are independent inhomogeneous Poisson processes with intensities given by the immigrant rates in Theorem
4.5
. Conditionally to
(\Pi^{m})_{m\in\bm{M}}
let independent
{P}\!{o}\!{i}\!{s}(\bm{\mathfrak{h}}^{\intercal})
clusters
G^{m}_{x}
with root of type
m
and born at time
x
for
m\in\bm{M}
and
x\in\Pi^{m}
. Then we have the following equality in distribution

(S^{m})_{m\in\bm{M}}\overset{d}{=}\big(\{s\in\mathds{R}\mid\exists m^{\prime}\in\bm{M},\exists x\in\Pi^{m^{\prime}},\exists u\in\mathcal{U}\ \text{such that}\ (u,m,s)\in G^{m^{\prime}}_{x}\}\big)_{m\in\bm{M}}

where
(S^{m})_{m\in\bm{M}}
is the
\bm{{\HAR}}_{\mathfrak{M}}\bm{(}-\infty\bm{)}
process from Theorem
3.9
.

Obtaining controls on clusters is crucial to derive properties for the whole process. For example, exponential bounds on clusters lead to exponential moments of number of points for the whole process, as explain in Section
5
.

Deriving results on clusters requires assumptions on the cluster functions
\mathfrak{h}^{m}_{m^{\prime}}
. As for classical Galton Watson processes, the relevant assumption is about the spectral radius of the matrix that collects the size of the interactions. Let us introduce the assumption in our case.

Assumption

(
Spec2
)
.

We say that
\mathfrak{M}\in\mathfrak{L}
satisfies
\bm{(}\text{{{Spec2}}}\bm{)}
if the spectral radius of the matrix
\mathfrak{H}_{\mathfrak{M}}
defined by (
4.7
) is smaller than one, equivalently
\spr(\mathfrak{H}_{\mathfrak{M}})<1
. We also require
\|h^{p}_{m}\|^{\infty}_{1,1}<\infty
for all
m\in\bm{M}
and all
p\in\bm{P}
.

Remark 4.10
.

Since the spectrum of a matrix and its transpose matrix is the same, in assumption
\bm{(}\text{{{Spec2}}}\bm{)}
it is equivalent to require
\spr\big(\mathfrak{H}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\intercal}\big)<1
, which is the condition to have subcritical clusters in
[
41
]
.

Under assumption
\bm{(}\text{{{Spec2}}}\bm{)}
one can already derive exponential controls on the number of points in a cluster. This result is a straightforward consequence of exponential moments for Galton Watson trees. An explicit bound, that depends on the matrix
\mathfrak{H}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\intercal}
is derived in Theorems 3.1 and 4.1 of
[
41
]
under the condition
\spr(\mathfrak{H}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\intercal})=\spr(\mathfrak{H}_{\mathfrak{M}})<1
.

5
Exponential moments of HAR processes

Obtaining good controls on the moments of a process is key point for both its theoretical study and applications. In this section we derive exponential moments for HAR processes. The main idea is to prove exponential moments them for HAR processes with linear parameters
\mathfrak{M}\in\mathfrak{L}
by taking advantage of cluster representation and then to transfer the result to general HAR processes by using a domination by a linear HAR process.

5.1
Linear domination of HAR processes

Given some parameters
\mathfrak{M}
we want to dominate a HAR process with parameters
\mathfrak{M}
by another HAR process with parameters
\mathfrak{M}^{+}\in\mathfrak{L}
. To do so, for any
\mathfrak{M}
we introduce the associated dominating parameters
\mathfrak{M}^{+}\in\mathfrak{L}
. Of course, the random drifts will also have to be slightly modified since we are not assuming positiveness of the random drifts
\xi=(\xi^{p}_{k})_{(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q}}
.

Definition 5.1

(
\mathfrak{L}
domination)
.

Let some parameters
\mathfrak{M}
, possibly nonlinear, given by

\mathfrak{M}=\big((\mu_{m})_{m},\ (h^{\alpha}_{\beta})_{\alpha,\beta},\ (\Phi^{\alpha})_{\alpha},\ (\kappa^{\alpha}_{p})_{\alpha,p}\big)

We define the parameters
\mathfrak{M}^{+}\in\mathfrak{L}
and the new random drifts by the following tabular.

\mathfrak{M}

\mu_{m}

h^{\alpha}_{\beta}

\Phi^{\alpha}

\kappa^{\alpha}_{p}

\xi^{p}_{k}

\mathfrak{M}^{+}

\nu_{m}:=\Phi^{m}(\mu_{m})

g^{\alpha}_{\beta}:=L_{\alpha}|h^{\alpha}_{\beta}|

\id

\varkappa^{\alpha}_{p}:=a^{\alpha}_{p}\id+|\kappa^{\alpha}_{p}(0)|

\zeta^{p}_{k}:=|\xi^{p}_{k}|

We denote
b^{\alpha}_{p}:=|\kappa^{\alpha}_{p}(0)|
and by convention we still have
\varkappa^{\alpha}_{p}(\varnothing)=0
.

For any initial condition
\mathfrak{C}
at time
t_{0}
, we define the initial condition
\mathfrak{C}^{+}
at time
t_{0}
by
\mathfrak{C}^{+,m}=\mathfrak{C}^{m}
, for all
m\in\bm{M}
and for all
(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q}
with
k<t_{0}
,
\mathfrak{C}^{+,p}_{k}=\varnothing
if
\mathfrak{C}^{p}_{k}=\varnothing
and
\mathfrak{C}^{+,p}_{k}=|\mathfrak{C}^{p}_{k}|
if
\mathfrak{C}^{p}_{k}\in\mathds{R}
.

The following proposition links HAR processes with parameters
\mathfrak{M}
(and random drifts
\xi
) and the ones with parameters
\mathfrak{M}^{+}
(and the random drifts
\zeta=|\xi|
). Recall that we denote by
\ell^{m}
the pre-intensity defined by (
2.3
) so that
\lambda^{m}=\Phi^{m}(\ell^{m})
.

Proposition 5.2

(
\mathfrak{L}
domination)
.

Let some parameters
\mathfrak{M}
and the associated
\mathfrak{M}^{+}
defined in Definition
5.1
.

1.

Suppose that assumption
\bm{(}\text{{{NoExpl}}}\bm{)}
[\mathfrak{M}]
holds, then
\bm{(}\text{{{NoExpl}}}\bm{)}
[\mathfrak{M}^{+}]
also holds. For any
t_{0}\in\mathds{R}
, for any integrable initial condition
\mathfrak{C}
, the initial condition
\mathfrak{C}^{+}
is also integrable and then the processes
\bm{{\HAR}}_{\mathfrak{M}}\bm{(}t_{0},\mathfrak{C},\xi\bm{)}
and
\bm{{\HAR}}_{\mathfrak{M}^{+}}\bm{(}t_{0},\mathfrak{C}^{+},\zeta\bm{)}
from Theorem
3.4
compare as follows:

–

\forall m,\ \forall t\geq t_{0},\ \lambda^{m}_{t}\leq(\lambda^{m}_{t})_{\mathfrak{M}^{+}}
and
L_{m}|\ell^{m}_{t}-\mu_{m}|\leq(\lambda^{m}_{t})_{\mathfrak{M}^{+}}-\Phi^{m}(\mu_{m})
,

–

\forall m,\ \forall C\subset[t_{0},\infty),\ S^{m}(C)\leq(S^{m}(C))_{\mathfrak{M}^{+}}
,

–

\forall(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q}
with
k\geq t_{0},\ |W^{p}_{k}|\leq(W^{p}_{k})_{\mathfrak{M}^{+}}.

2.

Suppose that assumptions
\bm{(}\text{{{Spec1}}}\bm{)}
[\mathfrak{M}]
and
\bm{(}\text{{{FiMe}}}\bm{)}
[\xi]
hold, then
\bm{(}\text{{{Spec1}}}\bm{)}
[\mathfrak{M}^{+}]
and
\bm{(}\text{{{FiMe}}}\bm{)}
[\zeta]
also hold and then the processes
\bm{{\HAR}}_{\mathfrak{M}}\bm{(}-\infty,\xi\bm{)}
and
\bm{{\HAR}}_{\mathfrak{M}^{+}}\bm{(}-\infty,\zeta\bm{)}
from Theorem
3.9
compare as follows:

–

\forall m,\ \forall t\in\mathds{R},\ \lambda^{m}_{t}\leq(\lambda^{m}_{t})_{\mathfrak{M}^{+}}
and
L_{m}|\ell^{m}_{t}-\mu_{m}|\leq(\lambda^{m}_{t})_{\mathfrak{M}^{+}}-\Phi^{m}(\mu_{m})
,

–

\forall m,\ \forall C\subset\mathds{R},\ S^{m}(C)\leq(S^{m}(C))_{\mathfrak{M}^{+}}
,

–

\forall(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q},\ |W^{p}_{k}|\leq(W^{p}_{k})_{\mathfrak{M}^{+}}.

This proposition allows us to transfer results on linear HAR processes to general HAR processes.

5.2
Exponential moments

In this subsection we use results on linear HAR processes, cluster representation and results on clusters, to derive exponential moments for linear HAR processes. Then we transfer these results to general HAR processes using the previous subsection. Having exponential moments cannot be achieved without an assumption on the random drifts. We will suppose the following sub-gaussian assumption.

Assumption

(
SubG
)
.

There exist constants
\mathfrak{e}_{p},\mathfrak{s}_{p}>0
for
p\in\bm{P}
such that for all
(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q}
, for all
t\geq 0
we have,

\mathds{E}\left[e^{t|\xi^{p}_{k}|}\right]\leq e^{\mathfrak{e}_{p}t+\mathfrak{s}_{p}t^{2}}.

Let
F=(F^{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\bm{M}\cup\bm{P}}
be non negative functions. For
X=(\lambda,S,W)
a HAR process we define
F\bullet S
and
F\bullet|W|
by,

\displaystyle F\bullet S=\sum_{m\in\bm{M}}\displaystyle\int_{\mathds{R}}F^{m}(t)dS^{m}_{t},\quad F\bullet|W|=\sum_{p\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{\mathds{R}}F^{p}(k)|W^{p}_{k}|d{D}^{p}_{k}.

Of course, one may omit the absolute values on
W
in case of a linear HAR process. Let
n\in\mathds{N}^{*}
. We also define the following vectors,

\displaystyle F^{\bm{M}}_{\infty,n}=\Big(\|F^{m}\|^{\infty}_{1,1/n}\Big)_{m\in\bm{M}},\quad F^{\bm{P}}_{\infty}=\Big(\|F^{p}\|^{\infty}_{1,n_{p}}\Big)_{p\in\bm{P}}.

We use the same notations as in
[
41
]
, so that for
\mathfrak{M}\in\mathfrak{L}
, the log Laplace transform of a
{P}\!{o}\!{i}\!{s}(\mathfrak{H}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\intercal})
Galton Watson tree is the function
\bm{{L}}:\mathds{R}_{+}^{\bm{M}}\to(\mathds{R}_{+}\cup\{\infty\})^{\bm{M}}
, defined by

\bm{{L}}(u)_{m}=\log\Big(\mathds{E}\left[e^{u\cdot\card_{\bm{M}}(\mathcal{T}^{m})}\right]\Big)

where
u\in\mathds{R}_{+}^{\bm{M}}
,
\mathcal{T}^{m}
is a
{P}\!{o}\!{i}\!{s}(\mathfrak{H}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\intercal})
Galton Watson tree with root of type
m\in\bm{M}
and
\card_{\bm{M}}(\mathcal{T}^{m})\in\mathds{N}^{\bm{M}}
is the vector containing the number of individuals of each type in
\mathcal{T}^{m}
.

Theorem 5.3

(Exponential moments for linear HAR processes)
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}\in\mathfrak{L}
and
(\lambda,S,W)
the HAR process
\bm{{\HAR}}_{\mathfrak{M}}\bm{(}-\infty\bm{)}
from Theorem
3.9
. Suppose that

•

The assumptions of Theorem
3.9
hold:
\bm{(}\text{{{Spec1}}}\bm{)}
[\mathfrak{M}]
and
\bm{(}\text{{{FiMe}}}\bm{)}
.

•

Assumptions
\bm{(}\text{{{Spec2}}}\bm{)}
[\mathfrak{M}]
and
\bm{(}\text{{{SubG}}}\bm{)}
hold (which implies assumption
\bm{(}\text{{{FiMe}}}\bm{)}
).

For any
n\in\mathds{N}^{*}
, let
R_{n}=\big(\|h^{p}_{m}\|^{\infty}_{1,1/n}\big)_{(m,p)\in\bm{M}\times\bm{P}}\times\big[(\Id-H^{W}_{W})^{-1}\big]^{\intercal}\prec\infty
and define

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{M}}=n\big(e^{\bm{{L}}(F^{\bm{M}}_{\infty,n})}-\bm{1}\big),

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{P}}=n\big(e^{\bm{{L}}(R_{n}F^{\bm{P}}_{\infty})}-\bm{1}\big),

where
\bm{{L}}
is the log Laplace function of a
{P}\!{o}\!{i}\!{s}(\mathfrak{H}_{\mathfrak{M}}^{\intercal})
Galton Watson process.
Then there exist
0<K<\infty
and a matrix
A_{\infty}
with non negative entries, both depending only on
\mathfrak{M}
such that the following holds for all
n\in\mathds{N}^{*}
,

\displaystyle\mathds{E}\left[e^{F\bullet S}\right]\leq\exp\Big[K\big(|\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{M}}|_{1}+|A_{\infty}\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{M}}|_{1}+|A_{\infty}\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{M}}|_{2}^{2}\big)\Big],

\displaystyle\mathds{E}\left[e^{F\bullet W}\right]\leq\exp\Big[K\big(|F^{\bm{P}}_{\infty}|_{1}+|\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{P}}|_{1}+|\tilde{F}^{\bm{P}}_{\infty}+A_{\infty}\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{P}}|_{1}+|\tilde{F}^{\bm{P}}_{\infty}+A_{\infty}\mathcal{X}_{n}^{\bm{P}}|_{2}^{2}\big)\Big],

with
\tilde{F}^{\bm{P}}_{\infty}:=\big[(\Id-H^{W}_{W})^{-1}\big]^{\intercal}F^{\bm{P}}_{\infty}
. Moreover, one can take

A_{\infty}=\big[(\Id-H^{W}_{W})^{-1}\big]^{\intercal}\big(a^{m}_{p}\|h^{m}_{p}\|^{\infty}_{1,n_{p}}\big)_{p,m\in\bm{P},\bm{M}}\prec\infty.

The assumptions are discussed after Corollary
5.5
, stating exponential moments for general parameters.

Remark 5.4
.

The function
\bm{{L}}
is deeply studied in
[
41
]
. Without diving into too much details, under assumption
