\bm{(}\text{{{Spec2}}}\bm{)}
, there exist
0<\theta_{0},\theta_{1}<\infty
, depending on
\mathfrak{M}
, such that if
|u|_{\infty}\leq\theta_{0}
then
|\bm{{L}}(u)|_{\infty}\leq\theta_{1}

[
41
]
. Thus for any
F
, as long as
F^{\bm{M}}_{\infty,n},F^{\bm{P}}_{\infty}<\infty
, one can find
\theta>0
such that
e^{\theta F\bullet S}
and
e^{\theta F\bullet W}
have finite expectation.

The
\ell^{2}
norms come from the term "
\mathfrak{s}_{p}t^{2}
" in assumption
\bm{(}\text{{{SubG}}}\bm{)}
. The matrix
A_{\infty}
correspond to the contribution of the random drifts to the immigrant rates in the cluster representation. The quantities
F^{\bm{M}}_{\infty,n}
(resp.
R_{n}F^{\bm{P}}_{\infty}
) summarize the total weights allocated to points in
F\bullet S
(resp.
F\bullet W
) in the cluster representation. Then the key quantities to calculate the exponential moments are respectively
\bm{{L}}(F^{\bm{M}}_{\infty,n})
and
\bm{{L}}(R_{n}F^{\bm{P}}_{\infty})
.

The entries of the matrix
R_{n}
and the vector
F^{\bm{M}}_{\infty,n}
are decreasing with
n\in\mathds{N}^{*}
so one can find the best trade-off in
n
between this decrease and the factor
n
in front of
\mathcal{X}^{\bm{M}}_{n}
and
\mathcal{X}^{\bm{P}}_{n}
.

From Theorem
4.5
it is clear that for a model
\mathfrak{M}\in\mathfrak{L}
, the stationary process
\bm{{\HAR}}_{\mathfrak{M}}\bm{(}-\infty\bm{)}
dominates all processes
\bm{{\HAR}}_{\mathfrak{M}}\bm{(}t_{0}\bm{)}
with
t_{0}\in\mathds{R}
. Thus this result is also valid for linear HAR processes started at time
t_{0}\in\mathds{R}
with empty initial condition. A similar result can be stated for HAR processes started at time
t_{0}
with an initial condition, up to moments assumptions on the initial condition.

The following result states exponential moments for general HAR processes. It is a direct consequence of the linear domination stated in Proposition
5.2
and the exponential moments for linear HAR processes stated in Theorem
5.3
.

Corollary 5.5

(Exponential moments)
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}
general parameters and
(\lambda,S,W)
the HAR process
\bm{{\HAR}}_{\mathfrak{M}}\bm{(}-\infty\bm{)}
from Theorem
3.9
. Suppose that

•

Assumptions
\bm{(}\text{{{Spec1}}}\bm{)}
[\mathfrak{M}]
and
\bm{(}\text{{{Spec2}}}\bm{)}
[\mathfrak{M}^{+}]
hold (
\mathfrak{M}^{+}
from Definition
5.1
).

•

Assumption
\bm{(}\text{{{SubG}}}\bm{)}
holds.

For any
n\in\mathds{N}^{*}
, define the matrix

R_{n}=\big(L^{p}\|h^{p}_{m}\|^{\infty}_{1,1/n}\big)_{(m,p)\in\bm{M}\times\bm{P}}\times\big[(\Id-H^{W}_{W})^{-1}\big]^{\intercal}.

Then, there exists
\theta_{0}>0
and
K<\infty
depending on
\mathfrak{M}
such that,

\forall n\in\mathds{N}^{*},\ \frac{|F^{\bm{M}}_{\infty,n}|_{\infty}}{\theta_{0}}=:\beta\leq 1\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\mathds{E}\left[e^{F\bullet S}\right]\leq e^{K(\beta n+\beta^{2}n^{2})},

and

\forall n\in\mathds{N}^{*},\ \frac{|R_{n}F^{\bm{P}}_{\infty}|_{\infty}}{\theta_{0}}=:\beta\leq 1\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\mathds{E}\left[e^{F\bullet|W|}\right]\leq e^{K\big[\beta n+\beta^{2}n^{2}+|F^{\bm{P}}_{\infty}|_{\infty}+|F^{\bm{P}}_{\infty}|_{\infty}^{2}\big]}.

Some remarks are in order.

Let
L\in\mathds{N}^{*}
. If one takes
F^{m}=\theta_{0}\mathds{1}_{[0,L)}
for all
m\in\bm{M}
, and choose
n=L
then it follows that

F\bullet S=\theta_{0}\sum_{m\in\bm{M}}S^{m}([0,L))=:\theta_{0}S([0,L)),

and
F^{\bm{M}}_{\infty,n}=\theta_{0}\bm{1}
. Thus we obtain,

\mathds{E}\left[e^{\theta_{0}S([0,L))}\right]\leq e^{KL^{2}}<\infty,

with
K
a constant depending on
\mathfrak{M}
. Similarly, there exists
\theta_{2}>0
depending on
\mathfrak{M}
, such that by taking
F^{p}(k^{\prime})=\theta_{2}\mathds{1}_{k^{\prime}=k}
for
k\in\mathcal{D}_{p}
, we obtain,

\sup_{(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathds{E}\left[e^{\theta_{2}|W^{p}_{k}|}\right]<\infty.

The
n^{2}
(or
L^{2}
) dependency is closely linked to the assumption
\bm{(}\text{{{SubG}}}\bm{)}
.

If
\bm{P}=\varnothing
, a HAR process is a classical Hawkes process and the assumptions are now reduced to
\spr(H^{S}_{S})<1
(since
H_{\mathfrak{M}}=\mathfrak{H}_{\mathfrak{M}^{+}}=H^{S}_{S}
in this case). Hence we recover the assumptions of
[
40
]
and
[
41
]
about exponential moments for Hawkes processes.

Now we focus on the main question, which is whether or not
\bm{(}\text{{{Spec1}}}\bm{)}
[
\mathfrak{M}
] (assumption to have existence of stationary processes) implies
\bm{(}\text{{{Spec2}}}\bm{)}
[
\mathfrak{M}^{+}
] (assumption to have exponential moments of clusters). It is the case for Hawkes processes since in this case
H_{\mathfrak{M}}=\mathfrak{H}_{\mathfrak{M}^{+}}=H^{S}_{S}
. For HAR processes it is more complex. Let parameters
\mathfrak{M}
such that
\bm{(}\text{{{Spec1}}}\bm{)}
[\mathfrak{M}]
holds, ie
\spr(H_{\mathfrak{M}})<1
with
H_{\mathfrak{M}}
defined in Definition
3.8
by

H_{\mathfrak{M}}=\begin{pmatrix}H^{S}_{S}&H^{S}_{W}\\[4.0pt]
H^{W}_{S}&H^{W}_{W}\end{pmatrix}.

Then
\bm{(}\text{{{Spec1}}}\bm{)}
[
\mathfrak{M}^{+}
] also holds with
\mathfrak{M}^{+}
by Proposition
5.2
. Assumption
\bm{(}\text{{{Spec2}}}\bm{)}
[\mathfrak{M}^{+}]
is
\spr(\mathfrak{H_{\mathfrak{M}^{+}}})<1
with
\mathfrak{H}_{\mathfrak{M}^{+}}
defined by (
4.7
). The bound (
4.5
) given in Theorem
4.5
implies that

\mathfrak{H}_{\mathfrak{M}^{+}}\preceq H^{S}_{S}+\tilde{H}^{S}_{W}(\Id-H^{W}_{W})^{-1}\tilde{H}^{W}_{S}

(5.1)

where
\tilde{H}^{S}_{W}=\Big(n_{p}L_{m}a^{m}_{p}\|h^{m}_{p}\|_{1}\Big)_{m,p}
and
\tilde{H}^{W}_{S}=\Big(L_{p}\|h^{p}_{m}\|_{1,1}^{\infty}\Big)_{p,m}
and
\preceq
means the
entry by entry comparison
. The difference between
(\tilde{H}^{S}_{W},\tilde{H}^{W}_{S})
and
(H^{S}_{W},H^{W}_{S})
is that when the classic
L^{1}
norm is used on one side, the discrete
L^{\infty}_{1}
norm is used on the other side. Indeed,

H^{S}_{W}=\Big(L_{m}a^{m}_{p}\|h^{m}_{p}\|_{1,n_{p}}^{\infty}\Big)_{m,p}\quad\text{and}\quad H^{W}_{S}=\Big(L_{p}\|h^{p}_{m}\|_{1}\Big)_{p,m}.

This is due to the fact that we are integrating in the past (between
-\infty
and
t
) to establish the existence of the stationary HAR processes (leading to
H_{\mathfrak{M}}
) while we are integrating in the future (between
s
and
\infty
) to describe clusters (leading to
\mathfrak{H}_{\mathfrak{M}^{+}}
).

On the one hand,
\tilde{H}^{S}_{W}
and
H^{S}_{W}
compare in the right sense:
\tilde{H}^{S}_{W}\preceq H^{S}_{W}
since
n_{p}\|\cdot\|_{1}\leq\|\cdot\|_{1,n_{p}}^{\infty}
but on the other hand, we have
\tilde{H}^{W}_{S}\succeq H^{W}_{S}
, the wrong sense, since
\|\cdot\|_{1}\leq\|\cdot\|_{1,1}^{\infty}
. Thus we cannot conclude on
\text{\hyperlink{spec}{$\bm{(}\text{{{Spec1}}}\bm{)}$}}[\mathfrak{M}]\Rightarrow\text{\hyperlink{specter}{$\bm{(}\text{{{Spec2}}}\bm{)}$}}[\mathfrak{M}^{+}]
. However, we are able to prove this comforting statement.

Proposition 5.6
.

Let parameters
\mathfrak{M}
. Then we have

\spr\left(\tilde{H}_{\mathfrak{M}}:=\begin{pmatrix}H^{S}_{S}&H^{S}_{W}\\[4.0pt]
\tilde{H}^{W}_{S}&H^{W}_{W}\end{pmatrix}\right)<1\ \ \Longrightarrow\ \ \text{\hyperlink{spec}{$\bm{(}\text{{{Spec1}}}\bm{)}$}}[\mathfrak{M}]\ \ \text{and}\ \ \text{\hyperlink{specter}{$\bm{(}\text{{{Spec2}}}\bm{)}$}}[\mathfrak{M}^{+}].

Remark 5.7
.

For
\mathfrak{M}\in\mathfrak{L}
, the condition
\spr(\tilde{H}_{\mathfrak{M}})<1
ensures both existence of the stationary process and subcritical clusters.

6
Asymptotic limits and application to concentration inequalities

In the study of stochastic processes, understanding whether or not a process converges to a stationary one plays a key role. A positive answer allows to transfer questions about the non stationary process into the study of the stationary one. It also opens the door to some creative ideas such as the coupling presented at the end of this section. This coupling ultimately leads to powerful concentration inequalities with numerous applications, such as in statistics.

6.1
Rate of convergence to the stationary state

Our main interest is about the convergence of HAR processes started at some time
t_{0}\in\mathds{R}
to the stationary HAR process. To remain as general as possible, the main theorem of this section states the convergence of two HAR processes with distinct initial conditions towards each other. Then one can specify this result with the empty initial condition and the stationary initial condition to obtain the convergence of the non stationary process to the stationary one. This is done in Corollary
6.4
.

Let us introduce the following assumption.

Assumption

(
ExpTail
)
.

We say that
\mathfrak{M}
satisfies assumption
\bm{(}\text{{{ExpTail}}}\bm{)}
if there exist constants
K,c>0
such that for all
\alpha,\beta\in\bm{M}\cup\bm{P}
, for all
x>0
,

|h^{\alpha}_{\beta}(x)|\leq Ke^{-cx}.

This tail assumption is the starting point to derive rate of convergence of two HAR processes with different initial conditions towards each other. This tail assumption transfers to coefficients and clusters functions introduced in Section
4
as explained in the following result.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let parameters
\mathfrak{M}\in\mathfrak{L}
, suppose that
\bm{(}\text{{{Spec1.1}}}\bm{)}
[\mathfrak{M}]
and
\bm{(}\text{{{ExpTail}}}\bm{)}
[\mathfrak{M}]
hold. Then there exist constants
K,c>0
such that

\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{p,k}{p',k'}_{\!\!\mathfrak{M}}\leq Ke^{-c(k-k^{\prime})},\quad\forall(p,k),(p^{\prime},k^{\prime})\in\mathcal{Q}\ \text{with}\ k^{\prime}\leq k

and

\mathfrak{h}^{m}_{m^{\prime}}(s,t)\leq Ke^{-c(t-s)},\quad\forall m,m^{\prime}\in\bm{M}\ \text{and}\ s<t.

The corner stone of this section is having precise control on the tail of clusters. Assumption
\bm{(}\text{{{ExpTail}}}\bm{)}
on the tails of the interaction functions leads to the following lemma, which is a specific case of Theorem 4.1 from
[
41
]
. As for point processes, for any cluster
G_{s}^{m}
born at time
s
with a root of type
m
, for any
I\subset\mathds{R}
, we denote by
G^{m}_{s}(I)
the total number of individuals of
G^{m}_{s}
whose birth dates are in
I
.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let parameters
\mathfrak{M}\in\mathfrak{L}
and suppose that assumptions
\bm{(}\text{{{ExpTail}}}\bm{)}
[\mathfrak{M}]
and
\bm{(}\text{{{Spec2}}}\bm{)}
[\mathfrak{M}]
hold. Let
G^{m}_{t_{0}}
be a
{P}\!{o}\!{i}\!{s}(\bm{\mathfrak{h}}^{\intercal})
cluster with root of type
m\in\bm{M}
and born at time
t_{0}\in\mathds{R}
. Then, there exists
\theta_{0}>0
depending on
\mathfrak{M}
such that for all
t\geq 0
,

\mathds{E}\left[e^{\theta_{0}G^{m}_{t_{0}}([t_{0}+t,\infty))}\right]\leq 1+K\exp(-ct)

for constants
c>0,\ K>0
depending on
\mathfrak{M}
but not on
m
nor
t_{0}
.

Given two initial conditions
\mathfrak{C}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{C}_{2}
at time
t_{0}
and
\delta>0
we define
|\mathfrak{C}_{1},\mathfrak{C}_{2}|_{\delta}
as follows,

|\mathfrak{C}_{1},\mathfrak{C}_{2}|_{\delta}:=\max\left(\left[\max_{m\in\bm{M}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t_{0}-}e^{-\delta(t_{0}-s)}d(\mathfrak{C}_{1}^{m}\vartriangle\mathfrak{C}_{2}^{m})_{s}\right],\ \left[\max_{p\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t_{0}-}e^{-\delta(t_{0}-k)}\psi(\mathfrak{C}^{p}_{1,k},\mathfrak{C}^{p}_{2,k})d{D}^{p}_{k}\right]\right)

with
\vartriangle
the symmetric difference on sets and
\psi
defined by

\psi(x,y):=\left\{\begin{aligned} &|x-y|\ \text{if}\ x,y\in\mathds{R}\\
&0\ \text{if}\ x=y=\varnothing\\
&|x|+1\ \text{if}\ x\in\mathds{R},\ y=\varnothing\\
&1+|y|\ \text{if}\ x=\varnothing,\ y\in\mathds{R}\end{aligned}\right..

(6.1)

The idea is to measure how different the two initial conditions are. Since the
\mathfrak{C}^{p}_{k}
are allowed to take both real values and the value
\varnothing
, we need the function
\psi
to deal with this two types of possible values. The following theorem is the main result of this section.

Theorem 6.3

(Asymptotic limit)
.

Let parameters
\mathfrak{M}
and suppose that assumptions
\bm{(}\text{{{NoExpl}}}\bm{)}
[\mathfrak{M}]
,
\bm{(}\text{{{Spec1.1}}}\bm{)}
[\mathfrak{M}]
,
\bm{(}\text{{{ExpTail}}}\bm{)}
[\mathfrak{M}]
and
\bm{(}\text{{{Spec2}}}\bm{)}
[\mathfrak{M}^{+}]
hold. Let
t_{0}\in\mathds{R}
and two integrable initial conditions,
\mathfrak{C}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{C}_{2}
, at time
t_{0}
for
\mathfrak{M}
. For
i=1,2
, we denote by

\Big((\lambda^{m,\mathfrak{C}_{i}})_{m\in\bm{M}},(S^{m,\mathfrak{C}_{i}})_{m\in\bm{M}},(W^{p,\mathfrak{C}_{i}})_{p\in\bm{P}}\Big)

the unique non exploding
\bm{{\HAR}}_{\mathfrak{M}}\bm{(}t_{0},\mathfrak{C}_{i}\bm{)}
process from Theorem
3.4
. There exists
\delta>0
depending only on
\mathfrak{M}
such that the following holds.

•

There exist
K,c>0
depending only on
\mathfrak{M}
such that for all
t\geq 0
,

\mathds{P}\Big(\forall m\in\bm{M},\ (S^{m,\mathfrak{C}_{1}}\vartriangle S^{m,\mathfrak{C}_{2}})\cap[t_{0}+t,\infty)=\varnothing\Big)\geq 1-K\mathds{E}\left[|\mathfrak{C}_{1},\mathfrak{C}_{2}|_{\delta}\right]e^{-ct},

\mathds{P}\bigg(\sum_{p\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}+t}^{\infty}|W^{p,\mathfrak{C}_{1}}_{k}-W^{p,\mathfrak{C}_{2}}_{k}|d{D}^{p}_{k}\leq e^{-ct}\bigg)\geq 1-K\mathds{E}\left[|\mathfrak{C}_{1},\mathfrak{C}_{2}|_{\delta}\right]e^{-ct}.

•

The following convergences hold a.s. and in
L^{q}
for
q\geq 1
such that there exists
q_{0}>q
satisfying
|\mathfrak{C}_{1},\mathfrak{C}_{2}|_{\delta}\in L^{q_{0}}
;

\sum_{m\in\bm{M}}\card\big((S^{m,\mathfrak{C}_{1}}\vartriangle S^{m,\mathfrak{C}_{2}})\cap[t+t_{0},\infty)\big)\xrightarrow[t\to\infty]{}0,

\sum_{p\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}+t}^{\infty}|W^{p,\mathfrak{C}_{1}}_{k}-W^{p,\mathfrak{C}_{2}}_{k}|d{D}^{p}_{k}\xrightarrow[t\to\infty]{}0.

Moreover, the rates of
L^{q}
convergence are at least
Ke^{-ct}
with
K,c>0
constants depending on
\mathfrak{M}
,
q
,
q_{0}
and
\mathds{E}\left[|\mathfrak{C}_{1},\mathfrak{C}_{2}|_{\delta}^{q_{0}}\right]
for
K
.

We obtained convergence, almost surely, in
L^{q}
and in probability, of
\bm{{\HAR}}_{\mathfrak{M}}\bm{(}t_{0},\mathfrak{C}_{i}\bm{)}
,
i=1,2
toward each other when time goes to infinity with explicit convergence rates. This shows that the asymptotic behavior does not depend on the initial condition and thus the process is decorrelated at infinity.

One can specify the result by taking for the first initial condition the empty initial condition and for the second one, the initial condition which is equal to the stationary HAR process. This allows to recreate the stationary HAR process through a HAR process with this particular initial condition. These two clever choices lead to the following result.

Corollary 6.4

(Convergence to the 1-stationary HAR process)
.

Let parameters
\mathfrak{M}
and suppose that assumptions
\bm{(}\text{{{Spec1}}}\bm{)}
[\mathfrak{M}]
,
\bm{(}\text{{{FiMe}}}\bm{)}
[\mathfrak{M}]
,
\bm{(}\text{{{ExpTail}}}\bm{)}
[\mathfrak{M}]
and
\bm{(}\text{{{Spec2}}}\bm{)}
[\mathfrak{M}^{+}]
hold. Let
t_{0}\in\mathds{R}
. We denote by

\Big((\lambda^{m,t_{0}})_{m\in\bm{M}},(S^{m,t_{0}})_{m\in\bm{M}},(W^{p,t_{0}})_{p\in\bm{P}}\Big)

the
\bm{{\HAR}}_{\mathfrak{M}}\bm{(}t_{0}\bm{)}
process from Theorem
3.4
and

\Big((\lambda^{m,-\infty})_{m\in\bm{M}},(S^{m,-\infty})_{m\in\bm{M}},(W^{p,-\infty})_{p\in\bm{P}}\Big)

the
\bm{{\HAR}}_{\mathfrak{M}}\bm{(}-\infty\bm{)}
process from Theorem
3.9
. Then the following holds.

•

There exist
K,c>0
depending on
\mathfrak{M}
such that for all
t\geq 0
,

\mathds{P}\Big(\forall m\in\bm{M},\ (S^{m,t_{0}}\vartriangle S^{m,-\infty})\cap[t+t_{0},\infty))=\varnothing\Big)\geq 1-Ke^{-ct},

\mathds{P}\bigg(\sum_{p\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}+t}^{\infty}|W^{p,t_{0}}_{k}-W^{p,-\infty}_{k}|d{D}^{p}_{k}\leq e^{-ct}\bigg)\geq 1-Ke^{-ct}.

•

The following convergences hold a.s. and, under assumption
\bm{(}\text{{{SubG}}}\bm{)}
, in
L^{q}
for all
q\geq 1

\sum_{m\in\bm{M}}\card\big((S^{m,t_{0}}\vartriangle S^{m,-\infty})\cap[t+t_{0},\infty))\big)\xrightarrow[t\to\infty]{}0,

\sum_{p\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}+t}^{\infty}|W^{p,t_{0}}_{k}-W^{p,-\infty}_{k}|d{D}^{p}_{k}\xrightarrow[t\to\infty]{}0.

Moreover, the speeds of
L^{q}
convergence are at least
Ke^{-ct}
with
K,c>0
constants depending on
\mathfrak{M}
and
q
.

This result links two of the most natural HAR processes: the stationary process and the process started at a fixed time
t_{0}
. They converge towards each other with exponential rates.

6.2
Berbee coupling, application to exponential concentration inequalities

For now, what remains unknown about HAR processes is whether or not they concentrate. More precisely, if functionals of the stationary HAR process concentrate around their means. For example, we can wonder if the total number of points in
[0,T)
divided by
T\in\mathds{N}^{*}
tends to the expectation of the number of points in
[0,1)
as
T
tends to
\infty
. More rigorously, do we have

\frac{S([0,T))}{T}=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{k=1}^{T}S([k-1,k))\longrightarrow\mathds{E}\left[S([0,1))\right]\ ?

By 1-stationary, for all
k\in\mathds{N}
we have
S([k-1,k))\sim S([0,1))
. Thus we only miss independence between the random variables
U_{k}=S([k-1,k))
to apply concentration inequalities. However, by Theorem
6.3
, we have decorrelation between
U_{k}
and
U_{k+h}
as
h\to\infty
. Indeed, Theorem
6.3
proves that memory fades exponentially fast. To make use of this "weak independence" structure we construct coupling
a la Berbee

[
6
]
. Similar techniques were used for stationary time series, see
[
58
,
51
,
23
]
.

Definition 6.5

(Berbee coupling)
.

Let parameters
\mathfrak{M}
. Suppose that assumption
\bm{(}\text{{{NoExpl}}}\bm{)}
[\mathfrak{M}]
holds. Let
n\in\mathds{N}^{*}
,
0<\ell\leq n
and
0\leq\phi<n
. Define the process

\mathbb{X}^{(n,\ell,\phi)}=\Big((\bblambda^{m,(n,\ell,\phi)})_{m\in\bm{M}},(\mathbb{S}^{m,(n,\ell,\phi)})_{m\in\bm{M}},(\mathbb{W}^{p,(n,\ell,\phi)})_{p\in\bm{P}}\Big)

by,
\forall j\in\mathds{Z}
, on
[jn+\phi,jn+n+\phi)
we have

\mathbb{X}^{(n,\ell,\phi)}=X^{jn+\phi-\ell}

where the process
X^{jn+\phi-\ell}
is the unique non exploding solution of
\bm{{\HAR}}_{\mathfrak{M}}\bm{(}jn+\phi-\ell\bm{)}
.

The idea of this construction is to create a process close to the stationary HAR process and with more explicit independence properties. To do so we start a process at time
jn+\phi-\ell
, we let it evolve during a time
\ell
and then we use it on
[jn+\phi,jn+n+\phi)
, and this for every
n\in\mathds{Z}
.

time
jn+\phi
(j+1)n+\phi
(j-1)n+\phi
\ell
n
\mathbb{X}^{(n,\ell,\phi)}

Fig 3:
Schematic representation of the coupling
\mathbb{X}^{(n,\ell,\phi)}
. The dotted part in red or green represent the unused part of the
\bm{{\HAR}}_{\mathfrak{M}}\bm{(}jn+\phi-\ell\bm{)}
processes. Only the solid lines are part of the coupling. The blue lines represent the independent random variables associated to
j-2
and
j
of Proposition
6.6
.

This coupling can be broken into independent blocks described in Proposition
6.6
bellow and illustrated in Figure
3
.

Proposition 6.6
.

Let
n\in\mathds{N}^{*}
,
0<\ell\leq n
,
0\leq\phi<n
and
\mathbb{X}^{(n,\ell,\phi)}
the coupling from Definition
6.5
. For any integer
j
we have,

\mathbb{X}^{(n,\ell,\phi)}{\Big|}_{[jn+\phi,\infty)}\ \mathchoice{\scalebox{1.4}{$\displaystyle\perp\!\!\!\!\perp$}}{\scalebox{1.4}{$\textstyle\perp\!\!\!\!\perp$}}{\scalebox{1.4}{$\scriptstyle\perp\!\!\!\!\perp$}}{\scalebox{1.4}{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp\!\!\!\!\perp$}}\ \mathcal{F}_{(jn+\phi-\ell)-}

where
\mathcal{F}_{s-}=\sigma\left(\bigcup_{r<s}\mathcal{F}_{r}\right)
.
Moreover, the random variables

\mathbb{X}^{(n,\ell,\phi)}{\Big|}_{[jn+\phi,(j+1)n+\phi+(n-\ell))}

for
j\in 2\mathds{Z}
are iid (independent and identically distributed). The same is true for
j\in 2\mathds{Z}+1
.

Proposition
6.6
is straightforward from the definition of the coupling. The second point shows how useful this coupling is in order to obtain concentration inequalities. Our coupling can be segmented into two interlaced sequences of iid random variables. However the two sequences are not independent between each others. The following proposition quantifies how close the stationary HAR process is from the coupling.

Proposition 6.7
.

Suppose that
\mathfrak{M}
satisfies assumptions
\bm{(}\text{{{Spec1}}}\bm{)}
[\mathfrak{M}]
,
\bm{(}\text{{{FiMe}}}\bm{)}
,
\bm{(}\text{{{ExpTail}}}\bm{)}
[\mathfrak{M}]
and
\bm{(}\text{{{Spec2}}}\bm{)}
[\mathfrak{M}^{+}]
. Let
n\in\mathds{N}^{*}
,
0<\ell\leq n
,
0\leq\phi<n
and
\mathbb{X}^{(n,\ell,\phi)}
the coupling from Definition
6.5
. Let also
X^{-\infty}
the stationary
\bm{{\HAR}}_{\mathfrak{M}}\bm{(}-\infty\bm{)}
process. Then there exist constants
K,c>0
depending on
\mathfrak{M}
only such that, for all integer
j
we have

\mathds{P}\Big(\forall m\in\bm{M},\ (\mathbb{S}^{m,(n,\ell,\phi)}\vartriangle S^{m,-\infty})\cap[jn+\phi,(j+1)n+\phi)=\varnothing\Big)\geq 1-Ke^{-c\ell}

and

\mathds{P}\left(\sum_{p\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{jn+\phi}^{((j+1)n+\phi)-}|\mathbb{W}^{p,(n,\ell,\phi)}_{k}-W^{p,-\infty}_{k}|d{D}^{p}_{k}\leq e^{-c\ell}\right)\geq 1-Ke^{-c\ell}.

This result is straightforward from Corollary
6.4
since we are comparing a non stationary process that has evolved a time
\ell
to the stationary process. The larger
\ell
is, the better the approximation of the stationary process by the coupling is, but the fewer independent pieces there are. Thus to prove concentration results for the stationary process there is a trade-off between how many independent pieces there are and how close the independent pieces are from the stationary process.

7
LASSO estimation for HAR processes

The collection of parameters
\mathfrak{M}
of a HAR process contains relevant information about the underlying real phenomena being modeled. The aim of this section is to construct a LASSO estimator of the interaction functions and the base rates. We use a LASSO procedure to select variables and recover only non zero interactions. This section is an adaptation of
[
28
]
and
[
54
]
.

7.1
Setting

To estimate the interactions with the LASSO procedure, we mainly follow the approach in
[
28
]
, with some differences due to the autoregressive part. In this sequel, we adopt the following setting.

•

We consider the case where the link functions
\Phi^{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in\bm{M}\cup\bm{P}
are known and are equal to the identity function.

•

The masks are also assumed to be known, without any other requirement except that the intensity must be non negative. Assuming the masks to be known makes sense for applications. Indeed, the choices of the masks are inherent to the experiment being conducted: what type of interactions do we want to detect? For example, one can take
\kappa(W)=|W|
if only the absolute value is relevant or
\kappa(W)=W\mathds{1}_{W>0}
if only positive signed
W
should interact.

•

The interaction functions are denoted
h^{\alpha,\star}_{\beta}
for
\alpha,\beta\in\bm{M}\cup\bm{P}
. We put an exponent
\star
to indicate that this parameter is unknown and has to be estimated. We are also assuming that the supports of the interaction functions are included in
(0,A]
with
A>0
known, and that they belong to
L^{2}((0,A])
.

•

The point processes base rates,
\mu^{\star}_{m}
for
m\in\bm{M}
are also among the parameters to be estimated.

•

The random drifts contains also relevant information since we do not assume them to be centered. From a statistical point of view their means are also relevant parameters. Thus we write

\xi^{p}_{k}=\mu^{\star}_{p}+\varepsilon^{p}_{k},\ (p,k)\in\mathcal{Q},

with
\mu^{\star}_{p}=\mathds{E}\left[\xi^{p}_{k}\right]
. We also denote
\sigma_{p}^{2}=\V(\xi^{p}_{k})=\V(\varepsilon^{p}_{k})
for
(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q}
.

We summarize the above consideration in
\mathfrak{M}^{\star}
containing all the true parameters,

\mathfrak{M}^{\star}=\big(\mu_{\alpha}^{\star},\ h^{\alpha,\star}_{\beta},\ \Phi^{\alpha}=\id,\ \kappa_{p}^{\alpha},\ \sigma_{p},\ A\big)_{\alpha,\beta\in\bm{M}\cup\bm{P},\ p\in\bm{P}},

(7.1)

and we suppose that
\bm{(}\text{{{Spec1}}}\bm{)}
[\mathfrak{M}^{\star}]
holds.
\bm{(}\text{{{FiMe}}}\bm{)}
also holds since we assume the existence of
\mu^{\star}_{p}
for
p\in\bm{P}
. Thus if
X=(\lambda,S,W)
is the
\bm{{\HAR}}_{\mathfrak{M}^{\star}}\bm{(}-\infty\bm{)}
process from Theorem
3.9
, it satisfies the following equations:

\displaystyle\lambda^{m}_{t}=\mu_{m}^{\star}+\sum_{m^{\prime}\in\bm{M}}\displaystyle\int_{t-A}^{t-}h^{m,\star}_{m^{\prime}}(t-s)dS^{m^{\prime}}_{s}+\sum_{p\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{t-A}^{t-}h^{m,\star}_{p}(t-k)\kappa^{m}_{p}(W^{p}_{k})d{D}^{p}_{k},

(7.2a)

\displaystyle S^{m}(C)=\displaystyle\int_{C\times\mathds{R}_{+}}\mathds{1}_{x\leq\lambda^{m}_{s}}d\pi^{m}(s,x),

(7.2b)

\displaystyle W^{p}_{k}=\varepsilon^{p}_{k}+\mu_{p}^{\star}+\sum_{m\in\bm{M}}\displaystyle\int_{k-A}^{k-}h^{p,\star}_{m}(k-s)dS^{m}_{s}+\sum_{p^{\prime}\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{k-A}^{k-}h^{p,\star}_{p^{\prime}}(k-k^{\prime})\kappa^{p}_{p^{\prime}}(W^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}})d{D}^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}.

(7.2c)

The following tabular summaries the known and unknown parameters.

known/unknown

known

unknown

unknown

known

parameter

\Phi^{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in\bm{M}\cup\bm{P}

\mu^{\star}_{m}
for
m\in\bm{M}

h^{\alpha,\star}_{\beta}
for
\alpha,\beta\in\bm{M}\cup\bm{P}

A

value/conditions

\id

\mu^{\star}_{m}\geq 0

h^{m,\star}_{m^{\prime}}\geq 0
and
h^{\alpha}_{\beta}\in L^{2}((0,A])

A<\infty

known/unknown

known

unknown

possibly unknown

parameter

\kappa^{\alpha}_{p}
for
\alpha\in\bm{M}\cup\bm{P}
and
p\in\bm{P}

\mu^{\star}_{p}=\mathds{E}\left[\xi^{p}_{k}\right]
for
p\in\bm{P}

\sigma^{2}_{p}=\V(\xi^{p}_{k})
for
p\in\bm{P}

value/conditions

\kappa^{m}_{p}\geq 0

We aim to estimate all unknown parameters denoted with an exponent
\star
from the observation over a time window of the process
X
, more precisely an observation
(S,W)
.

7.2
Oracle inequality

Let
T\geq 2
an integer and suppose that we are observing the process
X
on the time window
[-A,T)
, which means that we observe
X_{-A,T}=\Big[\big(S^{m}\cap[-A,T)\big)_{m\in\bm{M}},\ \big(W^{p}_{k},\ k\in\mathcal{D}_{p}\cap[-A,T)\big)_{p\in\bm{P}}\Big]
.

Let
\mathfrak{N}^{\star}
the true parameters to be estimated,

\mathfrak{N}^{\star}=(\mu_{\alpha}^{\star},h^{\alpha,\star}_{\beta})\quad\text{for}\quad\alpha,\beta\in\bm{M}\cup\bm{P}.

Finally, let
\Theta=\mathds{R}^{\bm{M}\cup\bm{P}}\times L^{2}((0,A])^{(\bm{M}\cup\bm{P})^{2}}
the set of all possible candidates for
\mathfrak{N}^{\star}
so that
\mathfrak{N}^{\star}\in\Theta
.

From equations (
7.2
) it is clear that there exist functions
\Psi^{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in\bm{M}\cup\bm{P}
such that

\lambda^{m}_{t}=\Psi^{m}(\mathfrak{N}^{\star},t,X_{t-A,t}),\quad m\in\bm{M},\ t\in\mathds{R},

W^{p}_{k}=\Psi^{p}(\mathfrak{N}^{\star},k,X_{k-A,k})+\varepsilon^{p}_{k},\quad p\in\bm{M},\ k\in\mathcal{D}_{p}.

Given
F=(F^{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\bm{M}\cup\bm{P}}
where
F^{m}:\mathds{R}\longrightarrow\mathds{R}
and
F^{p}:\mathcal{D}_{p}\longrightarrow\mathds{R}
define

(F\bullet X)_{[0,T)}=\sum_{m\in\bm{M}}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T-}F^{m}(t)dS^{m}_{t}+\sum_{p\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T-}F^{p}(k)W^{p}_{k}d{D}^{p}_{k}.

We also define the following norm

\|F\|_{[0,T)}^{2}=\sum_{m\in\bm{M}}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T-}F^{m}(t)^{2}dt+\sum_{p\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T-}F^{p}(k)^{2}d{D}^{p}_{k},

and the associated scalar product
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{[0,T)}
.

Let
\mathfrak{N},\mathfrak{N}^{\prime}\in\Theta
, since by linearity we have
\|\Psi(\mathfrak{N},X)-\Psi(\mathfrak{N}^{\prime},X)\|_{[0,T)}=\|\Psi(\mathfrak{N}-\mathfrak{N}^{\prime},X)\|_{[0,T)}
, we define the (pseudo and random) norm

\|\mathfrak{N}-\mathfrak{N}^{\prime}\|_{\Theta,T}=\|\Psi(\mathfrak{N},X)-\Psi(\mathfrak{N}^{\prime},X)\|_{[0,T)}.

With these notations we can introduce the following classical Least Square contrast
[
28
,
54
,
47
]
.

Definition 7.1

(Least square contrast)
.

For
\mathfrak{N}\in\Theta
, we define the contrast by,

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(\mathfrak{N})=

\displaystyle-2\sum_{m\in\bm{M}}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T-}\Psi^{m}(\mathfrak{N},t,X_{t-A,t})dS^{m}_{t}+\sum_{m\in\bm{M}}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T-}\Psi^{m}(\mathfrak{N},t,X_{t-A,t})^{2}dt

\displaystyle-2\sum_{p\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T-}\Psi^{p}(\mathfrak{N},k,X_{k-A,k})W^{p}_{k}d{D}^{p}_{k}+\sum_{p\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T-}\Psi^{p}(\mathfrak{N},k,X_{k-A,k})^{2}d{D}^{p}_{k}

With the above notations we have,

\mathcal{C}(\mathfrak{N})=-2(\Psi(\mathfrak{N},X)\bullet X)_{[0,T]}+\|\mathfrak{N}\|_{\Theta,T}^{2}.

Remark 7.2
.

The compensator of
\mathcal{C}(\mathfrak{N})
is

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}(\mathfrak{N})

\displaystyle=-2\left\langle\Psi(\mathfrak{N},X),\Psi(\mathfrak{N}^{\star},X)\right\rangle_{[0,T]}+\|\mathfrak{N}\|_{\Theta,T}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|\mathfrak{N}-\mathfrak{N}^{\star}\|_{\Theta,T}^{2}-\|\mathfrak{N}^{\star}\|_{\Theta,T}^{2}.

Therefore minimizing
\mathfrak{N}\mapsto\mathcal{C}(\mathfrak{N})
makes sense since the compensator of the contrast is, up to an additive constant, the (non observable) quantity of interest to minimize.

As in
[
28
]
, we introduce
\bm{\Phi}
a finite subset of
\Theta
, that we shall call a dictionary. In the sequel we only consider estimators among linear combinations of the dictionary, which means that any estimator will write as,

\hat{\mathfrak{N}}=\sum_{\varphi\in\bm{\Phi}}\hat{a}_{\varphi}\varphi=:\mathfrak{N}_{\hat{a}},\quad\hat{a}\in\mathds{R}^{\bm{\Phi}}.

The use of the dictionary
\bm{\Phi}
allows us to rewrite the contrast as a bilinear function of
a\in\mathds{R}^{\bm{\Phi}}
. Indeed since
\Psi
is linear in
\mathfrak{N}
we have

\mathcal{C}(\mathfrak{N}_{a})=-2a^{\intercal}b+a^{\intercal}Ga

where
b\in\mathds{R}^{\bm{\Phi}}
is a vector and
G\in\mathds{R}^{\bm{\Phi}^{2}}
is a matrix with coordinates given by

b_{\varphi}=(\Psi(\varphi,X)\bullet X)_{[0,T)},\quad\text{and}\quad G_{\varphi,\varphi^{\prime}}=\left\langle\Psi(\varphi,X),\Psi(\varphi^{\prime},X)\right\rangle_{[0,T)}.

For later use, we also define
\tilde{b}\in\mathds{R}^{\bm{\Phi}}
by

\tilde{b}_{\varphi}=\left\langle\Psi(\varphi,X),\Psi(\mathfrak{N}^{*},X)\right\rangle_{[0,T)},\quad\varphi\in\bm{\Phi}

so that
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}(\mathfrak{N})=-2a^{\intercal}\tilde{b}+a^{\intercal}Ga
.

The LASSO estimator consists minimizing the least square contrast plus an
L^{1}
penalty.
L^{1}
penalties are known to select variable, particularly when the dimension is large, as it may be the case here if
\bm{M}
or
\bm{P}
are large. As in
[
28
]
and
[
54
]
, we allow the weights to depend on the data to adapt and fit the level of noise
[
60
]
.

Definition 7.3

(LASSO estimator)
.

Let
\gamma>0
a tuning parameter and
d\in\mathds{R}^{\bm{\Phi}}
non negative weights, that might depend on the observation
X_{-A,T}
. We define
\hat{a}
by

\hat{a}\in\argmin_{a\in\mathds{R}^{\bm{\Phi}}}\big\{-2a^{\intercal}b+a^{\intercal}Ga+\gamma d^{\intercal}|a|\big\}

(7.3)

with
|a|=(|a_{\varphi}|)_{\varphi\in\bm{\Phi}}
. We also define
\hat{\mathfrak{N}}
the LASSO estimator of
\mathfrak{N}^{\star}
by

\hat{\mathfrak{N}}=\mathfrak{N}_{\hat{a}}.

(7.4)

The next step is to obtain theoretical guarantees on this estimator, and more precisely an oracle inequality.

To obtain oracle inequalities for the LASSO estimator we need two conditions. The first one is a control on the fluctuations of the contrast around its compensator. More precisely we have to control
b-\tilde{b}
. Given this control we can choose the weights
d
in consequence and
calibrate the LASSO

[
28
,
60
]
. The second condition is about invertibility like properties of the Gram matrix
G
, see
[
57
]
. In
[
28
]
the authors went for a full invertibility property; the Gram matrix is invertible and its smallest eigenvalue is
\eta>0
. Since requiring
G
to be invertible may be to restrictive, we turn instead on Restricted Eigenvalue (
\re
) properties which is a partial invertibility property as in
[
54
]
.
\re
property is a classical assumption in high dimensional LASSO procedure to prove oracle inequalities
[
57
]
. As opposed to
[
54
]
, we prove, in Section
7.3.2
that the
\re
holds with high probability. This has also been done for discrete time Hawkes processes in
[
47
]
using Kalikow decomposition.

For a vector
a\in\mathds{R}^{\bm{\Phi}}
and a subset of index
J\subset\bm{\Phi}
, we define the vector
a_{J}\in\mathds{R}^{\bm{\Phi}}
by
(a_{J})_{i}=a_{i}\mathds{1}_{i\in J}
.

Definition 7.4

(Restricted Eigenvalue property)
.

Let
\eta>0
,
c>0
and
s\in\mathds{N}
. We say that the matrix
G
satisfies the property
\bm{{\re}}\bm{(}\eta,c,s\bm{)}
if for all
a\in\mathds{R}^{\bm{\Phi}}
, for all subset
J\subset\bm{\Phi}
with
\card(J)\leq s
such that

|a_{J^{c}}|_{1}\leq c|a_{J}|_{1},

then the following holds,

a^{\intercal}Ga\geq\eta\|a_{J}\|^{2}.

Remark 7.5
.

If the dimension of
G
is large,
\re
property allows to get invertibility properties of
G
for vectors
a
whose
L^{1}
norms are mainly supported on
s
coordinates.

We can now state an oracle inequality for the LASSO estimator.

Theorem 7.6

(Oracle inequality)
.

Let
\gamma\geq 2
,
\eta>0
and
s\in\mathds{N}
. Then, on the event on which

(i)

\forall\varphi\in\bm{\Phi},\ |b_{\varphi}-\tilde{b}_{\varphi}|\leq d_{\varphi}
,

(ii)

G
satisfies the property
\bm{{\re}}\bm{(}\eta,c(\gamma),s\bm{)}
with
c(\gamma)=\frac{\gamma+2}{\gamma-2}
,

the following inequality is satisfied for the LASSO estimator with tuning parameter
\gamma
and weights
d
:

\|\hat{\mathfrak{N}}-\mathfrak{N}^{\star}\|^{2}_{\Theta,T}\leq\inf_{a\in\mathds{R}^{\bm{\Phi}},\ |\supp(a)|\leq s}\Big\{\|\mathfrak{N}_{a}-\mathfrak{N}^{\star}\|^{2}_{\Theta,T}+\eta^{-1}\big(\frac{\gamma+2}{2}\big)^{2}\sum_{\varphi\in\supp(a)}d_{\varphi}^{2}\Big\}.

(7.5)

Remark 7.7
.

Theorem
7.6
is only based on algebraic calculations on the definition of
\hat{a}
and has no links with the HAR background. Indeed, proving Theorem
7.6
is equivalent to showing that, under conditions
(i)
and
(ii)
, the following holds:

-2\hat{a}^{\intercal}\tilde{b}+\hat{a}^{\intercal}G\hat{a}\leq\inf_{a\in\mathds{R}^{\bm{\Phi}},\ |\supp(a)|\leq s}\Big\{-2a^{\intercal}\tilde{b}+a^{\intercal}Ga+\eta^{-1}\big(\frac{\gamma+2}{2}\big)^{2}\sum_{\varphi\in\supp(a)}d_{\varphi}^{2}\Big\}.

We refer to the proof of Theorem 8 of
[
54
]
for the proof. If
(i)
and
(ii)
are satisfied with high probability then (
7.5
) holds also with high probability. Having theoretical lower bounds on the probability of events
(i)
and
(ii)
, which are a bound on fluctuations around the compensator of the contrast and an
\re
property on
G
, requires complex probabilistic arguments on HAR processes. However, as long as conditions
(i)
and
(ii)
hold, inequality (
7.5
) holds and one can use it.

7.3
Lower bound on the Oracle event

This subsection is dedicated to prove lower bounds on the probability of events
(i)
and
(ii)
of Theorem
7.6
. To do so we need more assumptions.

First we assume the noises to be gaussian:
\varepsilon^{p}_{k}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{p}^{2})
. We also assume the standard deviations
\sigma_{p}
to be known, or at least that we know a lower and an upper bound
\underline{\sigma}
and
\overline{\sigma}
:

\forall p\in\bm{P},\ 0<\underline{\sigma}\leq\sigma_{p}\leq\overline{\sigma}<\infty.

(7.6)

Then we also suppose that assumptions
\bm{(}\text{{{Spec1}}}\bm{)}
[\mathfrak{M}^{\star}]
,
\bm{(}\text{{{Spec2}}}\bm{)}
[\mathfrak{M}^{\star,+}]
and
\bm{(}\text{{{ExpTail}}}\bm{)}
[\mathfrak{M}^{\star}]
hold. Since the random drifts are gaussian, it is also clear that
\bm{(}\text{{{FiMe}}}\bm{)}
and
\bm{(}\text{{{SubG}}}\bm{)}
hold.

Lastly, we specify the typical architecture of the dictionary
\bm{\Phi}
, more precisely we assume the following.

Assumption

(
Dict
)
.

The dictionary is elementary: each element
\varphi
of the dictionary
\bm{\Phi}
has only one coordinate which is non zero, and
\bm{\Phi}
is bounded by one,

\|\bm{\Phi}\|_{\infty}\leq 1

where

\|\bm{\Phi}\|_{\infty}=\max_{\varphi\in\bm{\Phi}}\ \max\{\|f\|_{\infty}\mid f\ \text{{coordinate of}}\ \varphi\}.

Here
f
is either a real number, in this case
\|f\|_{\infty}=|f|
, or a function, in this case
\|f\|_{\infty}
is the supremum norm of
f
.

Remark 7.8
.

Having an elementary dictionary is not mandatory but it makes things easier. One can have a non elementary dictionary by adapting the weights
d
. However, a bounded dictionary is crucial. Without loss of generality, we can suppose that
\bm{\Phi}
is bounded by
1
.

The above assumption allows us to decompose
\bm{\Phi}
as follows,

\bm{\Phi}=\bigcup_{\alpha\in\bm{M}\cup\bm{P}}\left(\bm{\Phi}_{\alpha}\cup\bigcup_{\beta\in\bm{M}\cup\bm{P}}\bm{\Phi}^{\alpha}_{\beta}\right)

where
\bm{\Phi}_{\alpha}
contains the elements
\varphi\in\bm{\Phi}
for which the only non zero coordiante is a base rate of type
\alpha
, ie
\varphi=\mu_{\alpha}
. Similarly,
\bm{\Phi}^{\alpha}_{\beta}
contains the elements
\varphi\in\bm{\Phi}
being an interaction function
h^{\alpha}_{\beta}
. Clearly, to avoid redundancy, we should have
\card(\bm{\Phi}_{\alpha})=1
for all
\alpha
. Let

\bm{\Phi}_{\bm{M}}=\bigcup_{m\in\bm{M}}\bm{\Phi}_{m},\quad\text{and}\quad\bm{\Phi}^{\bm{M}}=\bigcup_{m\in\bm{M},\alpha\in\bm{M}\cup\bm{P}}\bm{\Phi}^{m}_{\alpha}

and similarly

\bm{\Phi}_{\bm{P}}=\bigcup_{p\in\bm{P}}\bm{\Phi}_{p},\quad\text{and}\quad\bm{\Phi}^{\bm{P}}=\bigcup_{p\in\bm{P},\alpha\in\bm{M}\cup\bm{P}}\bm{\Phi}^{p}_{\alpha}.

We have divided the dictionary into four distinct subsets being, the point processes base rates, the chains base rates, the interaction functions on point processes and interaction functions on chains.

7.3.1
Bernstein inequalities to calibrate the LASSO

In this subsection we control the fluctuations of
b
around
\tilde{b}
. For
\varphi\in\bm{\Phi}
, let us write,

b_{\varphi}-\tilde{b}_{\varphi}=J_{\varphi,\bm{M}}+J_{\varphi,\bm{P}}

where

J_{\varphi,\bm{M}}=\sum_{m\in\bm{M}}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T-}\Psi^{m}(\varphi,t,X_{t-A,t})\big(dS^{m}_{t}-\lambda^{m}_{t}dt\big)

and

J_{\varphi,\bm{P}}=\sum_{p\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T-}\Psi^{p}(\varphi,k,X_{k-A,k})\varepsilon^{p}_{k}d{D}^{p}_{k}.

For
J_{\varphi,\bm{M}}
we use a Bernstein-type inequality for point processes given in Lemma
E.2
in Appendix
E
. The second term of
b_{\varphi}-\tilde{b}_{\varphi}
is the sum of a predictable process multiply by centered gaussian random variables. This term is controlled with, again, a Bernstein inequality adapted to this case stated in Lemma
E.3
in Appendix
E
.

Combining the results of Lemmas
E.2
,
E.3
leads to the following theorem that bounds the fluctuations of
b-\tilde{b}
with high probability.

Theorem 7.9

(Calibration of the LASSO)
.

There exists a constant
C^{\star}
depending only on
\mathfrak{M}^{\star}
such that for any
q>0
and any
\varphi\in\bm{\Phi}
, if

d_{\varphi}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &C^{\star}(q+1)\log(T)\sqrt{T}&&\text{if}\ \varphi\in\bm{\Phi}_{\bm{M}}\\
&C^{\star}(q+1)^{2}\log(T)^{2}\sqrt{T}&&\text{if}\ \varphi\in\bm{\Phi}^{\bm{M}}\\
&C^{\star}\sqrt{(q+1)\log(T)}\sqrt{T}&&\text{if}\ \varphi\in\bm{\Phi}_{\bm{P}}\\
&C^{\star}(q+1)^{3/2}\log(T)^{3/2}\sqrt{T}&&\text{if}\ \varphi\in\bm{\Phi}^{\bm{P}},\end{aligned}\right.

then we have,

\mathds{P}\left(|b_{\varphi}-\tilde{b}_{\varphi}|\leq d_{\varphi}\right)\geq 1-\frac{C^{\star}}{T^{q}}.

Remark 7.10
.

Depending on the nature of
\varphi
we have different order of magnitude for
d_{\varphi}
. It is due to the fact that if for example
\varphi
is a base rate then
\Psi(\varphi,X)
is not random, whereas
\Psi(\varphi,X)
is random if
\varphi
is an interaction function. Also, it is harder to control the martingale
dS^{m}_{t}-\lambda^{m}_{t}dt
than the gaussian noises
\varepsilon^{p}_{k}
which explains the other differences between the four cases.

7.3.2
Restricted Eigenvalue property for the Gram matrix

The goal of this subsection is to prove that
G
satisfies a
\re
property with high probability.

Recall that

G_{\varphi\varphi^{\prime}}=\sum_{m\in\bm{M}}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T-}\Psi^{m}(\varphi,t,X_{t-A,t})\Psi^{m}(\varphi^{\prime},t,X_{t-A,t})dt+\sum_{p\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T-}\Psi^{p}(\varphi,k,X_{k-A,k})\Psi^{p}(\varphi^{\prime},k,X_{k-A,k})d{D}^{p}_{k}

and define, with the notation of (
12.2
), the matrix
G_{0}^{1}
by

(G^{1}_{0})_{\varphi\varphi^{\prime}}=\sum_{m\in\bm{M}}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1-}\Psi^{m}(\varphi,t,X_{t-A,t})\Psi^{m}(\varphi^{\prime},t,X_{t-A,t})dt+\sum_{p\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1-}\Psi^{p}(\varphi,k,X_{k-A,k})\Psi^{p}(\varphi^{\prime},k,X_{k-A,k})d{D}^{p}_{k}.

The Restricted Eigenvalue property is stable under small perturbations in the following sense.

Proposition 7.11
.

Let
U
and
V
two square matrix and
\varepsilon>0
. Suppose that
U\in\bm{{\re}}\bm{(}\eta,c,s\bm{)}
and that

\forall i,j,\ |U_{ij}-V_{ij}|\leq\varepsilon.

Then
V\in\bm{{\re}}\bm{(}\eta-\varepsilon(c+1)^{2}s,c,s\bm{)}
.

Our strategy is to use the above result with
V=G
and
U=\mathds{E}\left[G\right]
, so that if
\mathds{E}\left[G\right]
satisfies a
\re
property and if
G
and
\mathds{E}\left[G\right]
are close enough, then
G
also satisfies a
\re
property. Thus we have to derive a concentration inequality for
G
, which is the purpose of Lemma
7.12
below.

Lemma 7.12

(Concentration for
G
)
.

Let
T=2n\tau+r
with integers
n\geq 2A+1
,
\tau>0
and
0\leq r<2n
. There exist constants
C^{\star}
and
C^{\star}_{q}
depending only on
\mathfrak{M}
and on
q
for
C^{\star}_{q}
such that for all
q>2
, for all
x>0
,

\mathds{P}\left(\big|G_{\varphi\varphi^{\prime}}-\mathds{E}\left[G_{\varphi\varphi^{\prime}}\right]\big|>Tx+TC^{\star}e^{-c^{\star}n}\right)\leq C^{\star}\tau e^{-c^{\star}n}+\frac{C^{\star}_{q}}{\tau^{q-1}}\Big(\frac{1}{x^{q}}+\frac{1}{x^{2(q-1)}}\Big).

Remark 7.13
.

G
is the sum of
T
variables distributed as
G^{1}_{0}
. To obtain concentration inequalities, classical results usually also require independence, which is not the case here. We use instead Berbee coupling, defined in Definition
6.5
, to obtain concentration and prove Lemma
7.12
, as explained in the proof section.

From stationarity,
\mathds{E}\left[G\right]=T\mathds{E}\left[G^{1}_{0}\right]
and thus
\mathds{E}\left[G\right]\in\bm{{\re}}\bm{(}T\eta,c,s\bm{)}\Leftrightarrow\mathds{E}\left[G_{0}^{1}\right]\in\bm{{\re}}\bm{(}\eta,c,s\bm{)}
. This observation combined to the key Lemma
7.12
, lead to the following theorem by taking
\tau\approx T^{1/2}
and
n\approx T^{1/2}
.

Theorem 7.14
.

Let
T\in\mathds{N}^{*}
, suppose that
\mathds{E}\left[G_{0}^{1}\right]\in\bm{{\re}}\bm{(}\eta_{0},c,s\bm{)}
with
\eta_{0}>0
. Let
q>1/2
and
0<\alpha<1
. Then if

T\geq C^{\star}\log^{2}\Big(\frac{C^{\star}(c+1)^{2}s}{\alpha\eta_{0}}\Big),\quad T\geq 8(A+1)^{2},

with probability at least

1-\card(\bm{\Phi})^{2}\frac{C^{\star}_{q}}{T^{q}}\bigg[1+\Big(\frac{(c+1)^{2}s}{\alpha\eta_{0}}\Big)^{4q}\bigg]

we have

G\in\bm{{\re}}\bm{(}(1-\alpha)\eta_{0}T,c,s\bm{)}

where
C^{\star},C^{\star}_{q},C^{\star}_{\gamma,q}
are constants depending on
\mathfrak{M}^{\star}
,
q
for the last two and
\gamma
for the last one.

In the previous result, we still need the assumption that
\mathds{E}\left[G_{0}^{1}\right]\in\bm{{\re}}\bm{(}\eta_{0},c,s\bm{)}
with
\eta_{0}>0
. However, it is not clear at all how to compute
\mathds{E}\left[G_{0}^{1}\right]
in order to check if the
\re
condition holds. Indeed, we do not know the true parameters and even if we knew them, we have no expression for the distribution (to compute the expectation) in terms of the true parameters. To bypass this difficulty, define the probability
\mathds{Q}
under which
(S^{m})_{m\in\bm{M}}
are independent homogeneous Poisson point processes of rates
1
after time
-A
and the chain
W^{p}_{k}
are independent
\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{p}^{2})
for
(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q}
with
k\geq-A
.

Then the following result links
\mathds{E}\left[G_{0}^{1}\right]
to
\mathds{E}_{\mathds{Q}}[G_{0}^{1}]
which is computable and depends only on
\bm{\Phi}
.

Proposition 7.15
.

Suppose that
\min_{m\in\bm{M}}\mu_{m}>0
and that
h^{\alpha}_{\beta}
are bounded for all
\alpha,\beta\in\bm{M}\cup\bm{P}
. Then there exists constants
C^{\star}
and
c^{\star}
depending only on
\mathfrak{M}^{\star}
such that, if
\mathds{E}_{\mathds{Q}}[G_{0}^{1}]\in\bm{{\re}}\bm{(}\eta,c,s\bm{)}
, we have, for all integer
T>0
, for all
B\geq 1
,

\mathds{E}\left[G_{0}^{T}\right]\in\bm{{\re}}\bm{(}Te^{-C^{\star}B^{2}}\big(\eta-C^{\star}(c+1)^{2}se^{-c^{\star}B}\big),c,s\bm{)}.

Therefore we have

\exists\eta_{1}>0,\ \mathds{E}_{\mathds{Q}}[G_{0}^{1}]\in\bm{{\re}}\bm{(}\eta_{1},c,s\bm{)}\ \Longrightarrow\ \exists\eta_{0}>0,\ \mathds{E}\left[G_{0}^{T}\right]\in\bm{{\re}}\bm{(}T\eta_{0},c,s\bm{)}

and for all
\alpha\in[0,1]
, we can chose

\eta_{0}\geq\eta_{1}(1-\alpha)\exp\bigg[-C^{\star}\Big(\log_{+}\Big[\frac{C^{\star}(c+1)^{2}s}{\alpha\eta_{1}}\Big]\Big)^{2}\bigg].

With this result in hand, one can compute
\mathds{E}_{\mathds{Q}}[G_{0}^{1}]
and check whether or not the
\re
condition holds. The choice of the dictionary is crucial since
\mathds{E}_{\mathds{Q}}[G_{0}^{1}]
depends only on
\bm{\Phi}
. If the
\re
condition is satisfied, then one can apply Theorem
7.14
and has a control on the event under which the oracle inequality is stated.

8
Proof of results of Section
3

8.1
Proof of Theorem
3.4

The proof of Theorem
3.4
consists of showing that the naive way of constructing the process does not fail. This way of constructing the process can be resumed as follows

Step 0:
For
t<t_{0}
the process is given by the initial condition
\mathfrak{C}
.

Step 1:
Calculate the value of the process at
t=t_{0}
.

Step 2:
If the process is constructed on
[t_{0},t_{1}]
then calculate the
temporary pre-intensities
at level
t_{1}
for
t_{1}<t\leq\min\{k\in\cup_{p\in\bm{P}}\mathcal{D}_{p}\mid k>t_{1}\}=:\mathcal{D}(t_{1})
defined by

\temp_{t_{1}}(\ell^{m})_{t}=\mu_{m}+\sum_{m^{\prime}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t_{1}}h^{m}_{m^{\prime}}(t-s)dS^{m^{\prime}}_{s}+\sum_{p\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t_{1}}h^{m}_{p}(t-k)\kappa^{m}_{p}(W^{p}_{k})d{D}^{p}_{k}.

The
temporary intensity
is defined by

\temp_{t_{1}}(\lambda^{m})_{t}=\Phi^{m}(\temp_{t_{1}}(\ell^{m})_{t}).

Basically,
\temp_{t_{1}}(\lambda^{m})_{t}
(resp.
\temp_{t_{1}}(\ell^{m})_{t}
) is the intensity (resp. pre-intensity) of node
m
at time
t
if there are no points (from any node) between
t_{1}
and
t
. Then one have access to the
pre-points at level
t_{1}
, being the atoms
(s,x)
of the Poisson random measures
\pi^{m}
such that
t_{1}<s\leq\mathcal{D}(t_{1})
and
0\leq x\leq\temp_{t_{1}}(\lambda^{m})_{t}
.

Step 3:
If there are no pre-points at level
t_{1}
it means that there is no points in
(t_{1},\mathcal{D}(t_{1})]
. In this case one can calculate with (
2.2c
) the chains
W^{p}_{k}
for which
k=\mathcal{D}(t_{1})
using what has already been constructed. The process is now fully constructed on
[t_{0},\mathcal{D}(t_{1})]
and one can apply Step 2 with
t_{1}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}(t_{1})
. If there are pre-points then one has to find the first one: the one with minimal time coordinate, if it exists. Let say this time coordinate is
t_{2}
. Then this pre-point with time coordinate
t_{2}
is in fact a true point and the process is fully construct on
[t_{0},t_{2}]
and one can apply Step 2 with
t_{1}\leftarrow t_{2}
.

The construction explained above may encounter two different problems, 1. it is possible that after an infinite number of steps it only constructs the process on a finite time interval, 2. it is also possible that there is no pre-point with minimal time coordinate in step 3, it might happen if there is an accumulation of pre-points. We will show that none of these happen.

Let
t^{*}>0
be small enough (the precise condition will be given at the end of the proof). Let

\{t_{0}<t_{1}<t_{2}<\cdots\}=(t^{*}\mathds{N}+t_{0})\cup\bigcup_{p\in\bm{P}}\mathcal{D}_{p}\cap[t_{0},\infty).

With this definition we have
t_{n}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
. We will show by induction on
n
that the total number of pre-points, with time coordinate in
[t_{0},t_{n}]
we will encounter through the construction is almost surely finite and that almost surely, for any
(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q}
with
t_{0}<\leq k\leq t_{n}
we have
|w^{p}_{k}|<\infty
. Thus the construction has to reach
t_{n}
in a finite number of steps and cannot fail. It will conclude the existence part of the proof since all true points are, at some point, pre-points, so non explosion will be clear.

Let us first explain how an
integrable
initial condition relates to assumption
\bm{(}\text{{{NoExpl}}}\bm{)}
. Let
f\in L^{1}
a non negative function then we have

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t}f(t-s)d\mathfrak{C}^{m}_{s}dt

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{b}\displaystyle\int_{a\vee s}^{b}f(t-s)dtd\mathfrak{C}^{m}_{s}

\displaystyle\leq\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{b}\|f\|_{1,[a-s,b-s]}d\mathfrak{C}^{m}_{s}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n\geq 0}\|f\|_{1,[a-b+n,n+1]}\card(\mathfrak{C}^{m}\cap[b-(n+1),b-n]).

Thus is
\mathfrak{C}
is an integrable initial condition,

\mathds{E}\left[\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t}f(t-s)d\mathfrak{C}^{m}_{s}dt\right]\leq K(a-b+2)\|f\|_{1}<\infty.

(8.1)

As a consequence,
\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t}f(t-s)d\mathfrak{C}^{m}_{s}dt<\infty
almost surely and
\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t}f(t-s)d\mathfrak{C}^{m}_{s}<\infty
almost surely almost everywhere.

Similarly, for any non negative
L^{1}
function
f
and non negative Lipschitz function
g
, we have

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t}f(t-k)g(\mathfrak{C}^{p}_{k})d{D}^{p}_{k}dt

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{b}\displaystyle\int_{a\vee k}^{b}f(t-k)g(\mathfrak{C}^{p}_{k})dtd{D}^{p}_{k}

\displaystyle\leq\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{b}\|f\|_{1,[a-k,b-k]}g(\mathfrak{C}^{p}_{k})d{D}^{p}_{k}

\displaystyle\leq\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{b}\|f\|_{1,[a-k,b-k]}\big[\lip(g)|\mathfrak{C}^{p}_{k}|+g(0)\big]\mathds{1}_{\mathfrak{C}^{p}_{k}\neq\varnothing}d{D}^{p}_{k}.

Thus for integrable initial condition,

\mathds{E}\left[\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t}f(t-k)g(\mathfrak{C}^{p}_{k})d{D}^{p}_{k}dt\right]<\infty.

(8.2)

With these results in hand, let us now prove the induction.

n=0
:
Almost surely there is no pre-points in the time interval
[t_{0},t_{0}]
since almost surely the Poisson random measure have no atoms lying on a given line. If
t_{0}=k
with
(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q}
then one can calculate
W^{p}_{k}
with (
2.2c
) and the initial condition. In this case we have
|W^{p}_{k}|<\infty
almost surely by (
8.1
) and (
8.2
).

n\rightarrow n+1
:
The process is constructed on
[t_{0},t_{n}]
and by induction, the total number of points before in
[t_{0},t_{n}]
is
\mathcal{F}_{t_{n}}
almost surely finite and all the
W^{p}_{k}
with
t_{0}\leq k\leq t_{n}
are also almost surely finite for
\mathcal{F}_{t_{n}}
. We can calculate the temporary pre-intensities at level
t_{n}
for
t_{n}<t\leq t_{n+1}
,

\temp_{t_{n}}(\ell^{m})_{t}=\mu_{m}+\sum_{m^{\prime}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t_{n}}h^{m}_{m^{\prime}}(t-s)dS^{m^{\prime}}_{s}+\sum_{p\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t_{n}}h^{m}_{p}(t-k)\kappa^{m}_{p}(W^{p}_{k})d{D}^{p}_{k}.

We denote by
E_{0}
the set of all pre-points at level
t_{n}=:s_{0}
, see Figure
4
.
Using the induction hypothesis,
\bm{(}\text{{{NoExpl}}}\bm{)}
, (
8.1
) and (
8.2
) we obtain that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{t_{n}}^{t_{n+1}}\left|\temp_{t_{n}}(\ell^{m})_{s}\right|ds

\displaystyle\leq t^{*}|\mu_{m}|+\sum_{m^{\prime}}\displaystyle\int_{t_{n}}^{t_{n+1}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t_{n}}|h^{m}_{m^{\prime}}(t-s)|dS^{m^{\prime}}_{s}dt

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\sum_{p\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{t_{n}}^{t_{n+1}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t_{n}}|h^{m}_{p}(t-k)||\kappa^{m}_{p}(W^{p}_{k})|d{D}^{p}_{k}dt

\displaystyle<\infty\ \ \mathcal{F}_{t_{n}}-a.s.

Since from
\bm{(}\text{{{NoExpl}}}\bm{)}
the
\Phi^{m}
are Lipschitz, it implies that

\mathds{E}\left[\card(E_{0})|\mathcal{F}_{t_{n}}\right]=\sum_{m}\displaystyle\int_{t_{n}}^{t_{n+1}}\temp_{t_{n}}(\lambda^{m})_{s}ds<\infty\ \ a.s.,

and so,
\mathcal{F}_{t_{n}}
-almost surely,
\card(E_{0})<\infty
.

t
\mathds{R}_{+}
t_{0}
t_{n}
=:s_{0}
s_{1}
t_{n+1}
\lambda^{m}_{t}
\temp_{t_{n}=s_{0}}(\lambda^{m})_{t}
\temp_{s_{1}}(\lambda^{m})_{t}

Fig 4:
This figure illustrates node
m
. In red there is
\lambda^{m}_{t}
, dotted in red there is the temporary intensity
\temp_{t_{n}}(\lambda^{m})_{t}
. Green dots are the pre-points of node
m
belonging to the set
E_{0}
. Step 2 is re-run for the first time at time
s_{1}
: time appearance of the first pre-point at level
s_{0}
(ie in
E_{0}
). Dotted in blue there is the temporary intensity at level
s_{1}
calculated in Step 2. The shaded area is where there might be some new pre-points (pre-points at level
s_{1}
that weren’t pre-points at level
s_{0}=t_{n}
), here there is one represented by a blue dot.

Of course the set
E_{0}
does not contain, in general, all the pre-points we are going to encounter in
[t_{n},t_{n+1}]
through the construction. Indeed, if
E_{0}\neq\varnothing
, we will apply again Step 2 of the construction at time
s_{1}
: the time of the first pre-point in
E_{0}
, then calculate temporary intensities at level
s_{1}
, then have a set
E_{1}
of pre-points at level
s_{1}
which might contains new pre-points: pre-points at level
s_{1}
that weren’t in
E_{0}
, ie that they weren’t pre-points at level
t_{n}=s_{0}
, see Figure
4
. And so on, we apply Step 2 at time
s_{2}
being the time of the first pre-point in
E_{1}
, calculate temporary intensities at level
s_{2}
, get the set
E_{2}
of pre-points at level
s_{2}
and maybe find some new pre-points: pre-points at level
s_{2}
but not at level
s_{1}
or
s_{0}
. The key of the reasoning is to stochastically dominate the number of new pre-points after each time we run Step 2 of the construction, ie the number of pre-points at level
s_{k}
but not at level
s_{k-1},\cdots,s_{0}
, that is
\card(E_{k}\setminus(E_{0}\cup\cdots\cup E_{k-1}))
. Each time Step 2 is run, the number of new pre-points follows a Poisson distribution of parameter being the sum, for each node
m
, of the area between
\temp_{s_{k}}(\lambda^{m})_{t}
and
\max_{i=0,\cdots,k-1}\temp_{s_{i}}(\lambda^{m})_{t}
when the first one is above the second one and
t\in]s_{k},t_{n+1}]
. For
k=1
, it is the sum for each node of the shaded zone area in Figure
4
.

Suppose that the pre-point which starts Step 2 is from node
m_{0}
and appears at time
s_{k}\in(t_{n},t_{n+1}]
. Then we have,

\displaystyle\left(\temp_{s_{k}}(\lambda^{m})_{t}-\max_{i=0,\cdots,k-1}\temp_{s_{i}}(\lambda^{m})_{t}\right)_{+}

\displaystyle=\left(\Phi^{m}(\temp_{s_{k}}(\ell^{m})_{t})-\max_{i=0,\cdots,k-1}\Phi^{m}(\temp_{s_{i}}(\ell^{m})_{t})\right)_{+}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\Phi^{m}(\temp_{s_{k}}(\ell^{m})_{t})-\Phi^{m}(\temp_{s_{k-1}}(\ell^{m})_{t})\right)_{+}

\displaystyle\leq L_{m}|\temp_{s_{k}}(\ell^{m})_{t}-\temp_{s_{k-1}}(\ell^{m})_{t}|

\displaystyle=L_{m}|h^{m}_{m_{0}}(t-s_{k})|.

Thus the area of the zones where there might be new pre-points is at most
\sum_{m}L_{m}\|h^{m}_{m_{0}}\|_{1,[0,t_{n+1}-s_{k}]}
. Taking the maximum over
m_{0}
and
s_{k}
, it comes that,

\#\{\text{new pre-points after Step 2}\}\leq\underbrace{{P}\!{o}\!{i}\!{s}\left(\max_{m}\sum_{m^{\prime}}L_{m^{\prime}}\|h_{m}^{m^{\prime}}\|_{1,[0,t^{*}]}\right)}_{\text{independently of previous Step 2's}}.

The independence with respect to all the previous iterations of Step 2 is crucial to obtain a Galton Watson structure. It comes from the fact that the areas of Poisson random measures taken into account for the new pre-points are areas that have never been visited before.

Thus, conditionally to
E_{0}
, the total number of pre-points in
(t_{n},t_{n+1}]
we are going to encounter through the construction is at most the cardinal of a forest of
\card(E_{0})
independent Galton-Watson processes with reproduction law
{P}\!{o}\!{i}\!{s}\left(\max_{m}\sum_{m^{\prime}}L_{m^{\prime}}\|h_{m}^{m^{\prime}}\|_{1,[0,t^{*}]}\right)
. Taking
t^{*}
small enough so that

\max_{m}\sum_{m^{\prime}}L_{m^{\prime}}\|h_{m}^{m^{\prime}}\|_{1,[0,t^{*}]}<1

ensures that all the Galton-Watson are sub-critical and so almost surely finite, which concludes the proof of the induction since
E_{0}
is also almost surely finite.

A quick word about uniqueness. If there is another non exploding
\bm{{\HAR}}_{\mathfrak{M}}\bm{(}t_{0},\mathfrak{C}\bm{)}
process then it must be the same at on
(-\infty,t_{0})
as the one we have just constructed since the initial conditions are the same. Then they must also be the same at
t_{0}
by applying the equations at time
t_{0}
. Then since there is a finite number of points until
t_{1}
, the temporary intensities must match until the first point after
t_{0}
, which then has to be the same for both of the processes. By doing so, one can easily check that this other solution must equal as the one we have just constructed on
[t_{0},t_{1}]
and so on.
∎

Remark 8.1
.

To prove Theorem
3.4
under assumptions
\bm{(}\text{{{NoExplBis}}}\bm{)}
and finite initial conditions, one only has to check that (
8.1
) and (
8.2
) still hold in this framework. The rest of the proof is the same.

8.2
Proof of Theorem
3.9

This subsection is dedicated to the proof of Theorem
3.9
, dealing with 1-stationary HAR processes. To construct them we use a Picard iteration whose scheme is defined as follows.

Initialisation :

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\lambda^{m,0}_{t}=\Phi^{m}(\mu_{m})\\
&S^{m,0}(C)=\displaystyle\int_{C}\displaystyle\int_{\mathds{R}_{+}}\mathds{1}_{x\leq\lambda^{m,0}_{t}}d\pi^{m}(t,x)\\
&W^{p,0}_{k}=\xi^{p}_{k}\\
\end{aligned}\right.

Iteration
n\to n+1
:

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\lambda^{m,n+1}_{t}=\Phi^{m}\bigg(\mu_{m}+\sum_{m^{\prime}\in\bm{M}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t-}h^{m}_{m^{\prime}}(t-s)dS^{m^{\prime},n}_{s}+\sum_{p\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t-}h^{m}_{p}(t-k)\kappa^{m}_{p}(W^{p,n}_{k})d{D}^{p}_{k}\bigg)\\
&S^{m,n+1}(C)=\displaystyle\int_{C}\displaystyle\int_{\mathds{R}_{+}}\mathds{1}_{x\leq\lambda^{m,n+1}_{t}}d\pi^{m}(t,x)\\
&W^{p,n+1}_{k}=\xi^{p}_{k}+\Phi^{p}\bigg(\sum_{m\in\bm{M}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{k-}h^{p}_{m}(k-s)dS^{m,n}_{s}+\sum_{p^{\prime}\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{k-}h^{p}_{p^{\prime}}(k-k^{\prime})\kappa^{p}_{p^{\prime}}(W^{p^{\prime},n}_{k^{\prime}})d{D}^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}\bigg).\end{aligned}\right.

Whenever one of the integral inside the link functions does not converge, we arbitrarily set its value to
0
. As we will see, it essentially never happens.

The proof can be divide into 4 steps. 1) Checking that the Picard iteration is well defined, 2) Proving that the Picard iteration converges almost surely and in
L^{1}
, 3) Showing that the limit is a solution to the HAR equations, 4) Showing uniqueness.

Let for
n\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\Lambda^{m}_{n}

\displaystyle=\sup_{t\in\mathds{R}}\mathds{E}\left[|\lambda^{m,n+1}_{t}-\lambda^{m,n}_{t}|\right]

\displaystyle\Gamma^{p}_{n}

\displaystyle=\sup_{k\in\mathcal{D}_{p}}\mathds{E}\left[|W^{p,n+1}_{k}-W^{p,n}_{k}|\right]

\displaystyle\Psi_{n}

\displaystyle=\max\Big(\sup_{m\in\bm{M},t\in\mathds{R}}\mathds{E}\left[|\lambda^{m,n}_{t}|\right],\sup_{(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathds{E}\left[|W^{p,n}_{k}|\right]\Big).

Step 1:
One can easily see that
\Psi_{0}<\infty
, it follows from assumption
\bm{(}\text{{{FiMe}}}\bm{)}
and the fact that
\lambda^{m,0}_{t}=\Phi^{m}(\mu_{m})
is deterministic. Thus we have

\displaystyle\mathds{E}\left[\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t-}|h^{m}_{m^{\prime}}(t-s)|dS^{m^{\prime},0}_{s}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathds{E}\left[\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t-}|h^{m}_{m^{\prime}}(t-s)|\lambda^{m^{\prime},0}_{s}ds\right]

\displaystyle\leq\Psi_{0}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t-}|h^{m}_{m^{\prime}}(t-s)|ds<\infty.

Thus, almost surely
\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t-}h^{m}_{m^{\prime}}(t-s)dS^{m^{\prime},0}_{s}
is well defined. Similarly, it is the case for any other integral involved in the definition of
\lambda^{m,1}_{t}
or
W^{p,1}_{k}
. Thus,
W^{p,1}_{k}
is well defined almost surely and
\lambda^{m,1}
is predictable and well defined
d\mathds{P}\left(\omega\right)\otimes dt
almost everywhere, which implies that
S^{m,1}
is well defined almost surely. Finally, one easily check that
\Psi_{1}\leq K(1+\Psi_{0})<\infty
with
K
a constant depending on
\mathfrak{M}
. Then an induction can be performed by applying the same reasoning and ensures that the Picard iteration is well defined and that
\Psi_{n}<\infty
for all
n\in\mathds{N}
.

Step 2:
The goal of this step is to show that

\boxed{\begin{aligned} &\forall m\in\bm{M},\ \forall t\in\mathds{R},\ \lambda^{m,n}_{t}\ \xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{a.s.\ \text{and}\ L^{1}}\lambda^{m}_{t}\in\mathds{R},\\
&\forall(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q},\ \ W^{p,n}_{k}\ \xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{a.s.\ \text{and}\ L^{1}}\ W^{p}_{k}\in\mathds{R},\\
&\sup_{m\in\bm{M},\ t\in\mathds{R}}\mathds{E}\left[|\lambda^{m}_{t}|\right]<\infty\ \text{and}\ \sup_{(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathds{E}\left[|W^{p}_{k}|\right]<\infty,\\
&\forall m,\ \forall C\subset\mathds{R}\ \text{bounded},\ S^{m,n}(C)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{a.s.\ \text{and}\ L^{1}}S^{m}(C)\in\mathds{N},\\
&\text{The almost sure event of convergence can be chosen}\\
&\text{uniform over all $(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q}$ and all $C\subset\mathds{R}$ bounded.}\end{aligned}}

Let
t\in\mathds{R}
and
n\in\mathds{N}^{*}
, since
\Phi^{m}
is
L_{m}
Lipschitz,

\displaystyle|\lambda^{m,n+1}_{t}-\lambda^{m,n}_{t}|

\displaystyle\leq L_{m}\bigg|\sum_{m^{\prime}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t-}h^{m}_{m^{\prime}}(t-s)\big(dS^{m^{\prime},n}_{s}-dS^{m^{\prime},n-1}_{s}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt+\sum_{p\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t-}h^{m}_{p}(t-k)\big(\kappa^{m}_{p}(W^{p,n}_{k})-\kappa^{m}_{p}(W^{p,n-1}_{k})\big)d{D}^{p}_{k}\bigg|.

We can rewrite the first integral in the RHS as follows,

\sum_{m^{\prime}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t-}\displaystyle\int_{\mathds{R}_{+}}h^{m}_{m^{\prime}}(t-s)\left(\mathds{1}_{\lambda^{m^{\prime},n-1}_{s}<x\leq\lambda^{m^{\prime},n}_{s}}-\mathds{1}_{\lambda^{m^{\prime},n}_{s}<x\leq\lambda^{m^{\prime},n-1}_{s}}\right)d\pi^{m^{\prime}}(s,x).

Noticing that

\left|\mathds{1}_{\lambda^{m^{\prime},n-1}_{s}<x\leq\lambda^{m^{\prime},n}_{s}}-\mathds{1}_{\lambda^{m^{\prime},n}_{s}<x\leq\lambda^{m^{\prime},n-1}_{s}}\right|=\mathds{1}_{\lambda^{m^{\prime},n-1}_{s}\wedge\lambda^{m^{\prime},n}_{s}<x\leq\lambda^{m^{\prime},n-1}_{s}\vee\lambda^{m^{\prime},n}_{s}}

it follows that,

\displaystyle\mathds{E}\left[|\lambda^{m,n+1}_{t}-\lambda^{m,n}_{t}|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{m^{\prime}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t}L_{m}|h^{m}_{m^{\prime}}(t-s)|\mathds{E}\left[|\lambda^{m^{\prime},n}_{s}-\lambda^{m^{\prime},n-1}_{s}|\right]ds

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt+\sum_{p\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t-}L_{m}|h^{m}_{p}(t-k)|\mathds{E}\left[|\kappa^{m}_{p}(W^{p,n}_{k})-\kappa^{m}_{p}(W^{p,n-1}_{k})|\right]d{D}^{p}_{k}.

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{m^{\prime}}L_{m}\|h^{m}_{m^{\prime}}\|_{1}\Lambda^{m^{\prime}}_{n-1}+\sum_{p\in\bm{P}}a^{m}_{p}L_{m}\|h^{m}_{p}\|_{1,n_{p}}^{\infty}\Gamma^{p}_{n-1}

Thus we obtain,

\Lambda^{m}_{n}\leq\sum_{m^{\prime}}L_{m}\|h^{m}_{m^{\prime}}\|_{1}\Lambda^{m^{\prime}}_{n-1}+\sum_{p\in\bm{P}}a^{m}_{p}L_{m}\|h^{m}_{p}\|_{1,n_{p}}^{\infty}\Gamma^{p}_{n-1}.

Similarly, one can show that

\Gamma^{p}_{n}\leq\sum_{m}L_{p}\|h^{p}_{m}\|_{1}\Lambda^{m}_{n-1}+\sum_{p^{\prime}\in\bm{P}}a^{p}_{p^{\prime}}L_{p}\|h^{p}_{p^{\prime}}\|_{1,n_{p^{\prime}}}^{\infty}\Gamma^{p^{\prime}}_{n-1}.

Let
\Lambda_{n}=(\Lambda^{m}_{n})_{m\in\bm{M}}
and similarly
\Gamma_{n}=(\Gamma^{p}_{n})_{p\in\bm{P}}
. The two previous equations rewrites

\begin{pmatrix}\Lambda_{n}\\
\Gamma_{n}\end{pmatrix}\leq H_{\mathfrak{M}}\begin{pmatrix}\Lambda_{n-1}\\
\Gamma_{n-1}\end{pmatrix}.

Iterating gives immediately

\begin{pmatrix}\Lambda_{n}\\
\Gamma_{n}\end{pmatrix}\leq(H_{\mathfrak{M}})^{n}\begin{pmatrix}\Lambda_{0}\\
\Gamma_{0}\end{pmatrix}.

Since we have

\Lambda^{m}_{0},\ \Gamma^{p}_{0}\leq\Psi_{0}+\Psi_{1}<\infty,

it follows from assumption
\bm{(}\text{{{Spec1}}}\bm{)}
[\mathfrak{M}]
that there exist constants
\spr(H_{\mathfrak{M}})\leq r<1
and
K>0
such that, for all
m\in\bm{M}
,
p\in\bm{P}
,

\forall n\in\mathds{N},\ \Lambda^{m}_{n},\ \Gamma^{p}_{n}\leq Kr^{n}.

(8.3)

Since the
0\leq r<1
, the series
\sum_{n}\Lambda^{m}_{n}
converges, and so,

\sum_{n}\mathds{E}\left[|\lambda^{m,n+1}_{t}-\lambda^{m,n}_{t}|\right]<\infty

which is the same as

\mathds{E}\left[\sum_{n}|\lambda^{m,n+1}_{t}-\lambda^{m,n}_{t}|\right]<\infty.

Therefore, almost surely,

\sum_{n}|\lambda^{m,n+1}_{t}-\lambda^{m,n}_{t}|<\infty

which implies that for all
t\in\mathds{R}
,

\lambda^{m,n}_{t}\ \xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{a.s.}\lambda^{m}_{t}\in\mathds{R}.

And the same reasoning gives,

\forall(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q},\ W^{p,n}_{k}\ \xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{a.s.}\ W^{p}_{k}\in\mathds{R}.

Since
\mathcal{Q}
is countable, the almost sure event of convergence can be taken uniformly over
\mathcal{Q}
.

From (
8.3
) and
\Psi_{0}<\infty
the
L^{1}
bounds and
L^{1}
convergences are clear.

Let us denote by
\vartriangle
the symmetric subtraction (
E\vartriangle F=(E\setminus F)\cup(F\setminus E)
for
E,F
sets). Let
j\in\mathds{N}^{*}
and
m\in\bm{M}
, then we have

\displaystyle\mathds{E}\left[\card\big((S^{m,n}\vartriangle S^{m,n+1})\cap[-j,j]\big)\right]

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\int_{[-j,j]}\mathds{E}\left[|\lambda^{m,n}_{s}-\lambda^{m,n+1}_{s}|\right]ds

\displaystyle\leq 2j\Lambda^{m}_{n}.

Since the cardinal only takes non negative integer values and because
\sum_{n}2j\Lambda^{m}_{n}<\infty
, by Borel-Cantelli Lemma, almost surely (uniformly in
j\in\mathds{N}^{*}
since the integers are countable), there exists
n_{0}=n_{0}(j,\omega)
such that for all
n\in\mathds{N}
, for all
m\in\bm{M}
,

S^{m,n_{0}+n}\cap[-j,j]=S^{m,n_{0}}\cap[-j,j].

Then, we can define
S^{m}
by
S^{m}\cap[-j,j]=S^{m,n_{0}(j)}\cap[-j,j]
for all
j
.

Finally, if
C\subset\mathds{R}
is bounded, then there exists
j\in\mathds{N}^{*}
such that
C\subset[-j,j]
, thus, almost surely (which does not depend on
C
), we have

S^{m,n}(C)=\card\big(S^{m,n}\cap[-j,j]\cap C\big)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\card\big(S^{m}\cap[-j,j]\cap C\big)=:S^{m}(C).

Step 3:
In the this step we show that the limit process found in the Step 2 is indeed a
\bm{{\HAR}}_{\mathfrak{M}}\bm{(}-\infty\bm{)}
process and is also 1-stationary.

First we prove that for all
m\in\bm{M}
, the set
S^{m}
matches its aimed value
\bar{S}^{m}
being the points under
\lambda^{m}
. One can observe that
\lambda^{m}
is predictable as limit of predictable terms. So, for
j\in\mathds{N}^{*}
and
m\in\bm{M}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathds{E}\left[\card\big((S^{m,n}\vartriangle\bar{S}^{m})\cap[-j,j])\big)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathds{E}\left[\displaystyle\int_{-j}^{j}\displaystyle\int_{\mathds{R}_{+}}\mathds{1}_{\lambda^{m,n}_{s}\wedge\lambda^{m}_{s}<x\leq\lambda^{m,n}_{s}\vee\lambda^{m}_{s}}d\pi^{m}(s,x)\right]

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\int_{-j}^{j}\mathds{E}\left[|\lambda^{m,n}_{s}-\lambda^{m}_{s}|\right]ds

\displaystyle\leq 2j\dfrac{Kr^{n}}{1-r}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}0.

Borel-Cantelli Lemma applies once again and we conclude that

a.s.,\ \forall j\in\mathds{N}^{*},\ S^{m}\cap[-j,j]=\bar{S}^{m}\cap[-j,j]

which implies that almost surely,
S^{m}=\bar{S}^{m}
.

Let us denote
\bar{\lambda}^{m}_{t}:=\Phi^{m}\bigg(\mu_{m}+\displaystyle\sum_{m^{\prime}\in\bm{M}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t-}h^{m}_{m^{\prime}}(t-s)dS^{m^{\prime}}_{s}+\sum_{p\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t-}h^{m}_{p}(t-k)\kappa^{m}_{p}(W^{p}_{k})d{D}^{p}_{k}\bigg)
. Let us now check that for all
m\in\bm{M}
we have

\lambda^{m}_{t}=\bar{\lambda}^{m}_{t}\quad d\mathds{P}\left(\omega\right)\otimes dt\ \ a.e.

Let
m\in\bm{M}
and
t\in\mathds{R}
, then,

\displaystyle\mathds{E}\left[|\lambda^{m,n}_{t}-\bar{\lambda}^{m}_{t}|\right]

\displaystyle\leq L_{m}\bigg(\sum_{m^{\prime}\in\bm{M}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t-}|h^{m}_{m^{\prime}}(t-s)|\mathds{E}\left[|\lambda^{m^{\prime},n}_{s}-\lambda^{m^{\prime}}_{s}|\right]ds+\sum_{p\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t-}|h^{m}_{p}(t-k)|a^{m}_{p}\mathds{E}\left[|W^{p,n}_{k}-W^{p}_{k}|\right]d{D}^{p}_{k}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq Cr^{n}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}0

with
C
a constant depending on
\mathfrak{M}
. Thus, for all
t\in\mathds{R}
we have
\mathds{P}\left(\lambda^{m}_{t}=\bar{\lambda}^{m}_{t}\right)=1
. Then, Fubini theorem implies that
d\mathds{P}\left(\omega\right)\otimes dt
almost everywhere,
\lambda^{m}_{t}=\bar{\lambda}^{m}_{t}
.
Similarly, almost surely, for all
(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q}
,

W^{p}_{k}=\xi^{p}_{k}+\Phi^{p}\bigg(\sum_{m}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{k-}h^{p}_{m}(k-s)dS^{m}_{s}+\sum_{p^{\prime}\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{k-}h^{p}_{p^{\prime}}(k-k^{\prime})\kappa^{p}_{p^{\prime}}(W^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}})d{D}^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}\bigg).

In the end we constructed a
\bm{{\HAR}}_{\mathfrak{M}}\bm{(}-\infty\bm{)}
process. It is also 1-stationary since in the Picard iteration the initial condition is 1-stationary and the evolution (through the Picard iteration) too, thus, the limit is also 1-stationary.

Step 4:
In this last step we show uniqueness. Consider two solutions, indexed by 1 and 2, and define

\beta^{m}=\sup_{t\in\mathds{R}}\mathds{E}\left[|(\lambda^{m}_{t})_{1}-(\lambda^{m}_{t})_{2}|\right]<\infty

and

\omega^{p}=\sup_{k\in\mathcal{D}_{p}}\mathds{E}\left[|(W^{p}_{k})_{1}-(W^{p}_{k})_{2}|\right]<\infty.

Then, using the equations (
2.2
), one has,

\mathds{E}\left[|(\lambda^{m}_{t})_{1}-(\lambda^{m}_{t})_{2}|\right]\leq\sum_{m^{\prime}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t-}L_{m}|h^{m}_{m^{\prime}}(t-s)|\beta^{m^{\prime}}ds+\sum_{p\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t-}L_{m}|h^{m}_{p}(t-k)|a^{m}_{p}\omega^{p}d{D}^{p}_{k}.

And thus,

\beta^{m}\leq\sum_{m^{\prime}}L_{m}\|h^{m}_{m^{\prime}}\|_{1}\beta^{m^{\prime}}+\sum_{p\in\bm{P}}a^{m}_{p}L_{m}\|h^{m}_{p}\|_{1n_{p}}^{\infty}\omega^{p}.

(8.4)

Similarly,

\omega^{p}\leq\sum_{m}L_{p}\|h^{p}_{m}\|_{1}\beta^{m}+\sum_{p^{\prime}\in\bm{P}}a^{p}_{p^{\prime}}L_{p}\|h^{p}_{p^{\prime}}\|_{1,n_{p^{\prime}}}^{\infty}\omega^{p^{\prime}}.

(8.5)

Thus if
\beta=(\beta^{m})_{m\in\bm{M}}
and
\omega=(\omega^{p})_{p\in\bm{P}}
, we have

\begin{pmatrix}\beta\\
\omega\end{pmatrix}\leq H_{\mathfrak{M}}\begin{pmatrix}\beta\\
\omega\end{pmatrix}.

Iterating this formula gives for all
n\in\mathds{N}^{*}
,

\begin{pmatrix}\beta\\
\omega\end{pmatrix}\leq(H_{\mathfrak{M}})^{n}\begin{pmatrix}\beta\\
\omega\end{pmatrix}.

And thus
\beta=0
and
\omega=0
. Since
\mathcal{Q}
is countable, a.s.,
\forall(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q},\ (W^{p}_{k})_{1}=(W^{p}_{k})_{2}
. It also implies that
d\mathds{P}\left(\omega\right)\otimes dt
almost everywhere,
(\lambda^{m}_{t})_{1}=(\lambda^{m}_{t})_{2}
. Finally, one also has,

\displaystyle\mathds{E}\left[\card\big(S^{m}_{1}\vartriangle S^{m}_{2}\big)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathds{E}\left[\displaystyle\int_{\mathds{R}}|(\lambda^{m}_{t})_{1}-(\lambda^{m}_{t})_{2}|dt\right]

\displaystyle\leq\displaystyle\int_{\mathds{R}}\beta^{m}dt

\displaystyle=0.

Thus, a.s.,
\forall m,\ S^{m}_{1}=S^{m}_{2}
.
∎

9
Proof of results of Section
4

9.1
Proof of Lemma
4.3
and Proposition
4.4

Given a sequence
Z
it is clear that the sequence
V
is well defined by the (
4.1
). Unrolling (
4.1
) gives an expression of
V^{p}_{k}
as a fixed linear combination of the
Z^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}
with
k^{\prime}\leq k
, which proves existence of the coefficients. Uniqueness is obtained by setting the sequence
Z
to
(\mathds{1}_{(p,k)=(p_{0},k_{0})})_{(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q},\ k\geq k_{0}}
since then associated
V
sequence satisfies

V^{p}_{k}=\sum_{p^{\prime}\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{k_{0}}^{k}\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{p,k}{p',k'}_{\!\!\mathfrak{M}}Z^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}d{D}^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}=\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{p,k}{p_0,k_0}_{\!\!\mathfrak{M}}

which proves uniqueness in
point 1
since
V
is well defined and forces the value of the coefficient.

We now prove
point 3
. The fact that
\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{p_1,k_0}{p_0,k_0}_{\!\!\mathfrak{M}}=\mathds{1}_{p_{0}=p_{1}}
is quite clear, take
Z=(\mathds{1}_{(p,k)=(p_{0},k_{0})})_{(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q},\ k\geq k_{0}}
. On one side, by
point 1
we have

\displaystyle V^{p}_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{p^{\prime}\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{k_{0}}^{k}\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{p,k}{p',k'}_{\!\!\mathfrak{M}}Z^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}d{D}^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{p,k}{p_0,k_0}_{\!\!\mathfrak{M}},

thus we have

V^{p_{1}}_{k_{0}}=\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{p_1,k_0}{p_0,k_0}_{\!\!\mathfrak{M}}.

On the other side, again by
point 1
we have

\displaystyle V^{p_{1}}_{k_{0}}

\displaystyle=Z^{p_{1}}_{k_{0}}+\sum_{p^{\prime}\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{k_{0}}^{k_{0}-}a^{p_{1}}_{p^{\prime}}h^{p_{1}}_{p^{\prime}}(k_{0}-k^{\prime})V^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}d{D}^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=Z^{p_{1}}_{k_{0}}

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}_{p_{0}=p_{1}},

which concludes on
\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{p_1,k_0}{p_0,k_0}_{\!\!\mathfrak{M}}=\mathds{1}_{p_{0}=p_{1}}
.

Let
(Z^{p}_{k})_{(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q},\ k\geq k_{0}}
be an arbitrary sequence, let
(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q}
with
k>k_{0}
. Then

\displaystyle V^{p}_{k}

\displaystyle=Z^{p}_{k}+\sum_{p^{\prime\prime}\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{k_{0}}^{k-}a^{p}_{p^{\prime\prime}}h^{p}_{p^{\prime\prime}}(k-k^{\prime\prime})V^{p^{\prime\prime}}_{k^{\prime\prime}}d{D}^{p^{\prime\prime}}_{k^{\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle=Z^{p}_{k}+\sum_{p^{\prime\prime}\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{k_{0}}^{k-}a^{p}_{p^{\prime\prime}}h^{p}_{p^{\prime\prime}}(k-k^{\prime\prime})\left[\sum_{p^{\prime}\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{k_{0}}^{k^{\prime\prime}}\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{p'',k''}{p',k'}_{\!\!\mathfrak{M}}Z^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}d{D}^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}\right]d{D}^{p^{\prime\prime}}_{k^{\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle=Z^{p}_{k}+\sum_{p^{\prime}\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{k_{0}}^{k-}\left[\sum_{p^{\prime\prime}\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{k^{\prime}}^{k-}a^{p}_{p^{\prime\prime}}h^{p}_{p^{\prime\prime}}(k-k^{\prime\prime})\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{p'',k''}{p',k'}_{\!\!\mathfrak{M}}d{D}^{p^{\prime\prime}}_{k^{\prime\prime}}\right]Z^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}d{D}^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}.

By identification of the coefficients one obtains
point 3
.
Point 2
, aka the 1-periodicity, can be proved by looking at the sequence
\tilde{Z}
: the version of
Z
shifted by
1
. The associated
\tilde{V}
sequence is then the sequence
V
shifted by
1
and thus the coefficients must be 1-periodic. It can also be proved by induction for increasing values of
k-k^{\prime}
by using the formula of
point 3
.
∎

It is quite clear that the closed form satisfies the recursive property of point 3 of Lemma
4.3
. Indeed, one only needs to cut the paths in two sub paths: all the first steps except the last one and the last one. Thus the closed form must be equal to the coefficients.
∎

9.2
Proof of Theorem
4.5

To prove Theorem
4.5
we solve completely the recurrence relation on the chains by using coefficients and then inject back in the intensity equation these calculations.
L^{1}
bounds on the cluster functions are derived with analytics properties of the coefficients derived in Lemma
B.1
in Appendix
B
.

We will prove the theorem for
t_{0}=-\infty
. If
t_{0}\in\mathds{R}
the ideas are the same and even less technical since no integrability conditions are needed. Recall that
\kappa^{p}_{p^{\prime}}(x)=a^{p}_{p^{\prime}}x+b^{p}_{p^{\prime}}
. Let

Z^{p}_{k}:=\xi^{p}_{k}+\sum_{m\in\bm{M}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{k-}h^{p}_{m}(k-s)dS^{m}_{s}+\sum_{p^{\prime}\in\bm{P}}b^{p}_{p^{\prime}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{k-}h^{p}_{p^{\prime}}(k-k^{\prime})d{D}^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}

so that

\displaystyle W^{p}_{k}

\displaystyle=\xi^{p}_{k}+\sum_{m\in\bm{M}}\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{k-}h^{p}_{m}(k-s)dS^{m}_{s}+\sum_{p^{\prime}\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{k-}h^{p}_{p^{\prime}}(k-k^{\prime})(a^{p}_{p^{\prime}}W^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}+b^{p}_{p^{\prime}})d{D}^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=Z^{p}_{k}+\sum_{p^{\prime}\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{k-}a^{p}_{p^{\prime}}h^{p}_{p^{\prime}}(k-k^{\prime})W^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}d{D}^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}

One can calculate

\sup_{(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathds{E}\left[|Z^{p}_{k}|\right]\leq\sup_{p,k}\mathds{E}\left[\xi^{p}_{k}\right]+\sum_{m}(\sup_{p}\|h^{p}_{m}\|_{1})\sup_{s\in\mathds{R}}\mathds{E}\left[|\lambda^{m}_{s}|\right]+\sum_{p^{\prime}\in\bm{P}}\sup_{p}b^{p}_{p^{\prime}}\|h^{p}_{p^{\prime}}\|_{1,n_{p^{\prime}}}^{\infty}.

Thus,
\sup_{(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathds{E}\left[|Z^{p}_{k}|\right]<\infty
by the different assumptions. Proposition
B.2
, in Appendix
B
, extends point 1 of Lemma
4.3
to random sequences and to the case
k_{0}=-\infty
. Since by Theorem
3.9
we have
\sup_{(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathds{E}\left[|W^{p}_{k}|\right]<\infty
, combined to
\sup_{(p,k)\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathds{E}\left[|Z^{p}_{k}|\right]<\infty
we can apply Proposition
B.2
and we obtain that,

\displaystyle W^{p}_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{p^{\prime}\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{k}\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{p,k}{p',k'}_{\!\!\mathfrak{M}}Z^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}d{D}^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\Hd^{b}_{p,k}(t_{0})+\Hd^{\xi}_{p,k}(t_{0})+\sum_{m}\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{k-}\Hd^{m}_{p,k}(t_{0},t)dS^{m}_{t},

which proves the first result of Theorem
4.5
. Then one has just to inject this expression into the
\lambda^{m}_{t}
equation (
2.2a
) to get the second result. It remains to check the
L^{1}
bound.

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}^{m}_{m_{0}}(s,t)

\displaystyle=h^{m}_{m_{0}}(t-s)+\sum_{p,p^{\prime}\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{s+}^{t-}\displaystyle\int_{s+}^{k}a^{m}_{p}h^{m}_{p}(t-k)\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{p,k}{p',k'}_{\!\!\mathfrak{M}}h^{p^{\prime}}_{m_{0}}(k^{\prime}-s)d{D}^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}d{D}^{p}_{k}

\displaystyle=h^{m}_{m_{0}}(t-s)+\sum_{p,p^{\prime}\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{s+}^{\infty}\displaystyle\int_{s+}^{\infty}\mathds{1}_{k^{\prime}\leq k<t}a^{m}_{p}h^{m}_{p}(t-k)\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{p,k}{p',k'}_{\!\!\mathfrak{M}}h^{p^{\prime}}_{m_{0}}(k^{\prime}-s)d{D}^{p}_{k}d{D}^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}.

Thus,

\|\mathfrak{h}^{m}_{m_{0}}(s,\cdot)\|_{1}\leq\|h^{m}_{m_{0}}\|_{1}+\sum_{p,p^{\prime}\in\bm{P}}\displaystyle\int_{s+}^{\infty}\displaystyle\int_{s+}^{\infty}a^{m}_{p}\|h^{m}_{p}\|_{1}\mathds{1}_{k^{\prime}\leq k}\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{p,k}{p',k'}_{\!\!\mathfrak{M}}h^{p^{\prime}}_{m_{0}}(k^{\prime}-s)d{D}^{p}_{k}d{D}^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}.

(9.1)

Lemma
B.1
in Appendix
B
states analytics results on the coefficients, in particular on sums of the coefficients. Fix
p,p^{\prime}\in\bm{P}
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{s+}^{\infty}\displaystyle\int_{s+}^{\infty}\mathds{1}_{k^{\prime}\leq k}

\displaystyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{p,k}{p',k'}_{\!\!\mathfrak{M}}h^{p^{\prime}}_{m_{0}}(k^{\prime}-s)d{D}^{p}_{k}d{D}^{p^{\prime}}_{k^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k_{0}\in\mathcal{D}_{p^{\prime}}\cap(s,s+1]}\sum_{n\in\mathds{N}}\displaystyle\int_{k_{0}+n}^{\infty}\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{p,k}{p',k_0+n}_{\!\!\mathfrak{M}}h^{p^{\prime}}_{m_{0}}(k_{0}+n-s)d{D}^{p}_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k_{0}\in\mathcal{D}_{p^{\prime}}\cap(s,s+1]}\sum_{n\in\mathds{N}}\displaystyle\int_{k_{0}}^{\infty}\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{p,k}{p',k_0}_{\!\!\mathfrak{M}}h^{p^{\prime}}_{m_{0}}(k_{0}+n-s)d{D}^{p}_{k}

\displaystyle\leq\|h^{p^{\prime}}_{m_{0}}\|_{1,1}^{\infty}\sum_{k_{0}\in\mathcal{D}_{p^{\prime}}\cap(s,s+1]}\displaystyle\int_{k_{0}}^{\infty}\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{p,k}{p',k_0}_{\!\!\mathfrak{M}}d{D}^{p}_{k}

\displaystyle\leq\|h^{p^{\prime}}_{m_{0}}\|_{1,1}^{\infty}\times n_{p}\big[(\Id-H^{W}_{W})^{-1}\big]_{pp^{\prime}}

where we use, the fact that
\mathcal{D}_{p^{\prime}}=\sqcup_{n\in\mathds{Z}}(\mathcal{D}_{p^{\prime}}\cap(0,1]+n)
, 1-periodicity of the coefficients and point 2 of Lemma
B.1
. Combining this bound with (
9.1
) concludes the proof.
∎

There is actually nothing to prove, the expression of
\lambda^{m}
given in Theorem
4.5
is clear.
∎

10
Proof of results of Section
5

We first prove the linear domination established in Proposition
5.2
, then we prove exponential moments for linear HAR processes established in Theorem
5.3
. To prove Corollary
5.5
we combine Proposition
5.2
and Theorem
5.3
. finally we prove Proposition
5.6
.

10.1
Proof of Proposition
5.2
